A BOOK FOR MARRIED WOMEN. --

The information contained in this book ought to be known by every married woman, and it will not harm the unmarried to read. The book is conveniently divided into 12 C hapters. The first chapter treats of the changes at puberty, or when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a doctor's standpoint ; points out thci best age for marriage, and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful information that one can ordinarily get only from an intelligent doctor. The third chapter treats of the marriage of blood relations; and condemns such marriages as a rule. Chapter four treats of the signs of pregnancy. The fifth chapter tells how a woman should live during the pregnant state. The sixth chanter treats of mishaps and how to avoid them. The seventh chapter treats of maternal impressions and shows that birthmarks are not clue to longings t n the part of the mother, bat rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter teaches how to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that women should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that confinements can be made comparatively easy I certain rules are obeyed ; these rules are given. The ninth chapter treats of the proper management of oonfinemen until the baby is born. The tenth chapter tells bow to treat the mother till she is up and about again. The eleventh chapter treats of sterility ; civet) the main causes of it, how these may be overcome and children result The last chapter treats of the "change,' a most important article for al women over forty. The book is full of useful information and no book is written which goes so thoroughly into matters relating to married women. Some may think too much i told ; such can scarcely be the case, for knowledge is power and the means of attaining happiness. The book can be had from Dr. T. R. ALLINSON, 25 Box, 4, Spanish-place, Manchester-square, London, W., in return for a Posta Order for Is. Id.

" CAPITAL" CHAT.

A good story is getting into circulation about Lord Cross. It has been said that he got his seat in the Government as the Queen's adviser and favourite. This was the excuse given to an agnostic wag,who does not view with much favour any resuscitation of the remnants of the " old gang." "Yes, yes," he replied. "but you should call him Victoria Cross; and have done with it. lie has earned the distinction, and he looks con- scious of it." The description seems likely to be regarded as appropriate.

The Tied House Tenants' League, which has been slowly maturing, is now in virile activity. Its executive believes that it will play a most impor- tant part in the coming elections. Some experience was gained at Oxford, and in the Norfolk election and it is to be put to good use. The tied house tenant knows that in any system of reduction or closing he will not obtain a penny of compensation as things are, and so be fights against the Veto Bill. Promise him a prior claim to the brewer, and the case is different. To obtain promises from candi- dates is the one great aim of the League.

--- The military experts in this country will be pleased to bear that some of our cordite ammuni- tion has been ordered by Germany in order to test its merits. An impreseiou prevails already that it is better than the German smokeless powder,though it may affect the barrels of the rifles more disad- vantageously. The Chitral campaign has been of good service in proving the value of cordite, but the great unsettled problem is how it will keep. On this point the evidence is in conclusive, and I am told by an expert that the best kinds will not keep St all.

The workers in the various London constituen- cies share Lord Tweedmouth's caution about big meetings. Some of tilem purposely stayed away from the mighty gathering in the Albert Hall in order to hold small meetings in the streets, to can- vass by-streets, and to forward the work of their respective elections. The County Council cam- paign was a lesson in the over-estimation of big public meetings. The Progressives never had better meetings followed by a worse poll. The various Conservative candidates in London are doing a good deal of canvassing, and not much else, unless it be the putting in an appearance at small " smokers " in the various public-houses.

The personal relics of Lord Nelson in the posses- sion ot Viscount Bridport are announced for sale by auction. They will not, lam informed, include the papers and correspondence of the great com- mander. I hear, indeed,that steps have been taken with the object of acquiring these interesting docu- ments for the nation. Prominent members of the late Government have moved in order to avoid the dispersion of the collection. In regard to the relics, even some of these may be secured for the country, in which case Greenwich Hospital, where the coat and vest worn by Nelson at Trafalgar are preserved, would probably become their home.

As the election struggle approaches, the Union- ists are not so confident of victory as they were when, a few days ago, they were challenging the late Government to appeal to the people. Ido not hear so much now of sweeping the country and coming back with an enormous majority This cocke,uren.ss, to use an expressive term of the late Professor Huxley, was brought about,as regards the London Unionists, by the victories they achieved in the County Council elections in the beginning of the .; ear, and was fomented by the effect those victories n ad upon the Unionist members in Parlia- ment. The triumphs led to a lorresponding des- pondency in the mind of the Liberals, who had fought desperately to maintain their old supre- macy. That despondency has now been super- seded by trust in what Sir William llarcourt calls the good old cause. How the attack on the House .of Lords will be viewed by middle-class tradesmen it is impossible to forecast. The West-end section of tbem will probably vote for the Unionists,on the principle that they will support the institution • -which brings tnem business.

It is becoming evident that the Unionists are in- clined to climb down as regards Mr. Gully and the Speakership. The first note is sounded by the tact that it is agreed to be a hopeless job to contest Carlisle. The next point is that Sir Matthew White Ridley is very much in love with the Home Office he has not had any hanging business nor dyna- mite to deal with yet),and he wishes to stay there if the Unionists come into power. The third point is even more instructive. If anyone were put in -opposition to Mr. Gully for the Speakership there are certain to be in the new lionse those who would not record their votes scrsinst him. Lord Salisbury has a man in reserve tor the Speakership —Mr. J. W. Lowther—yet he does not come for- ward as such, and Mr. Stuart Wortley, another left out in the cold, would not dream of such a thing.

--- The movement which is likely to be set on foot for erecting a memorial to Professor liuxley, to be placed in the natural history collection alongside the busts of Darwin and Professor Owen, will meet with general approval both inside and outside the ranks of scientific men. I believe some men eminent in science have taken the matter in hand, and will form themselves into a small provisional -committee with the view of bringing the proposal before the public. In his controversies the Pro- fessor made no enemies, and unless the subscrip- tion is limited to a given sum the funds may flow in with an abundance which may prove embarrass- ing.

The notion that the Duke of Cambridge has been treated unfairly—forced to resign, in tact—is re- peated in the " World," but this repetition of a curious story gives it no authority. The duke had hinted at his own resignation before his resigna- tion was announced, and Sir Henry Campbell- Bannerman put no pressure on him at all. Seeinr, that the House of Commons was always wanting to know when the Rartington Report was to be •carried out, the duke spontaneously sent in his paprs. He was more afraid of Mr. A. C. Morton than the Minister of War. What has vexed his apologists is that the papers were not returned, with a request that he would go on as before—a re- quest no Government dare make.

I had a long conversation the other day with some Conservative and Liberal Unionist election agents in the North of London, and from all that I could gather from them the Liberal Unionist party, so far as they come in:o contact with it, is break- ing down—in other words, it is rapidly becoming ab,orbed by the Liberal party. As both frankly admitted the fact, and that, too, with some bitter- ness on the part of the Liberal Unionist representa- tive, and disgust on the part of the Con=ervative agent, the canker seems to have eaten rather deeply into Liberal Unionism. The vete@ of these repent- ant prodigals will accordingly be given to the Radical candidates. The reasons assigned by these returning vagrants from the Liberal fold are—firA, absolute repugnance to coalescing with the Tories; second, a growing belief that Home Rule is right after all; and thirdly, a fixed determination to refuse to support a Government opposed to dises- tablishment and the reform of the House of Lords. I have no doubt that similar experiences will have been encountered in other constituencies through- out the country. They prove that Liberal Union- ism cannot survive a course of sound Liheral legis- lation, and the fate of the party outside Mr. Chamberlain's area of personal influence in the Midlands would seem to be that the more timid will sink into Toryism and be lost, and the more staunch, who still believe in disestahlishment and Liberal develcpment, will return to the Liberal ranks. Mr. W. C. Bonnerjee, the Liberal candidate for Barrow-in-Furness, is an interesting personality. i ' He was born in India, n a suburb of Calcutta, and was called to the Bar by the Middleyemple.London, in June. 1867. lie returned to India in 1868, and practised there. His family have all been educated In this country. While in India he acted. occasion- ally as junior counsel to the Crown, and in 1878 was apPointed a Fellow of Calcutta University, and dur- ing the present year he has been practising before Judicial Committee of the Privy Council in Indian appeals. He is an advanced Radical, and is fifty-one years of age. His candidature is arousing great enthusiasm.

BOWLING.

LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE BOWLING ASSOCIATION. THE AMATEUR CHAMPIONSHIP.

Much first-class trundling was witnessed at Prestwich, on Saturday, on the occasion of the sixth annual indivi- dual merit competition. The contest attracted a large concourse of spectators, and the interest of the proceed- ings were enhanced by the presence of the Marquis of Lorne and a party of friends, who partook of luncheon with the president and officers of the county association. In the course of the afternoon three past champions suc- ceeded in earning a like number of victories, but two gold medalists, who gained their honours in Yerkshire and Lancashire,dropped their colours to their adversaries for the time being. The entire of the first round (60 events) were disposed of under the guidance of the presi- dent (Meakin and Coombes), who acted as referees. Score : _

.THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, J

THE WOMEN AT HOME.

They at down to dinner—husband, wife, and three young children, all hearty, happy, and well dressed. It was seven o'clock. The meal was just on time, as meals should be.

"Aren't you tired, my dear P" said the husband, kindly. "Perhaps! might say yes if I thought about it," answered the wife; "but I really don't feel tired." And yet that woman had been on her feet, working herself, and superintending half a dozen servants, for twelve hours at least. She had a big house to look after, many callers to see and that lot of things to do which count but cannot be counted. Still, she was young, strong, and cheerful, and so didn't mind.

That is one thing and a good thing too. But the women who are entitled to credit are the ones who don't usually get it—women who do all she did, and do it under pressure—who are on time just the same and have to fight every inch of the way.

Here is one of this splendid sort. She says :"I kept up with my daily duties, but felt no pleasure in the work I had in hand." Why not ? Because she was burdened with illness, as so many are, who deserve a better fortune.

This—Mrs. Peckham, of 40, Dorset.st., London,says : "Oft and on I have suffered for twenty years from biliousness and indigestion. At first I was attacked with sickness of the stomach,frequently expectorating a gase- ous or watery fluid. I had no relish for food,and what I ate gave me pain and distress at the chest and sides. As time went on these symptoms grew worse. I became weak and nervous, and lost much sleep. I kept up with my daily duties, but felt no pleasure in the work I had in hand. I tried first one medicine and then another, taking charcoal and all the other drugs recommended for bad digestion, but nothing gave me more than tem- porary relief.

"In this way I continued to suffer year after year, sometimes better and at other times worse. In the early part of this year my brother-in-law, of South Hackney, told me of a medicine called Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup. I had also heard the Syrup highly spoken of by others, which induced me to try it. I pro- cured a supply from Mr. Taylor, Chemist, Hanover- place, Regent's Park, and after having taken it for a week, felt great relief. Soon my food digested and the sickness arsd water-brash left me, and I now feel better than I have felt for years. I am desirous that other sufferers may know the valul of Mother Seigel 's Syrup, and therefore give my full permission t6O the publication of this statement. (Signed) M. E. Peckham."

Soncebody well says that it is not the way in which we meet great crises, but the way we bear the daily grind and labour of life that tells the story of courage and character. Yes, indeed. And what multitude of faith- ful patient wives and mothers do under these circum- stances where one day is like ten. They carry their aches and pains without giving up until they are weighted beyend all further endurance, and are just obliged to give up. To all these there is no friend or benefactor in England, private or public, to compare with Mother , Seigel, who suffered and toiled in the same way-10c. tors and drugs being useless—until she found a medicine to cure herself. Thank Mercy ! she did find it at last. What a blessed work it has been doing in this island for the past ten or fifteen years is the sequel to the story,and hosts of w3men know it by heart. Yes, and more are learning it with every sun that rises, from every tongue that can talk.

CELEBRITIES' PHOTOGRAPHS WHICH

SELL BEST.

BEAUTY FIRST, BRAINS NOWFIERE. . .

There is probably more comparison effected between the beauty of women than between any other things which own their original either to Nature or—let it be whispered with caution—to the efforts of the artificer. And a good deal of beauty is not merely skin deep, but of the shop-window order. Indeed, in order to measure accurately the popularity of well-known persons either as beauties or as people of brain, it is almost imperative to take a run round the shop-windows of the photograph sellers and note the folk whose counterfeit presentments are exhibited in the greatest variety. This is exactly what I have been doing (writes a representative of " Cassell's Saturday Journal,") and an interview with a busy dealer in photographs shows to a trustworthy extent the bent of the public inclination. " Let us first take the photographs of politicians," I began, " leaving heauty—no offence—on one side for the moment. Whose photograph sells best ?" " Well, I should say that Mr. Balfour' has the widest sale just now, though more photographs have been sold of Mr. Gladstone than of any other living man. Mr. Chamberlain sells well also, but the small sale of Lord Rosebery'sphotograph is accounted for by the fact that he has a rooted objection to being confronted with the camera." "Now, whose photograph sells best of all ?" "The Queen's—by far, followed closely by the Princess of Wales's. But the most popular photograph of recent years is the well-known one of the four generations— her Majesty, the Prince of Wales, the Duke of York and his infant son. More of these have been sold than of any other photographs during the past few years " It is interesting to note, by the way, that over 300,000 copies have been sold of the now somewhat ancient photograph of the Princess of Wales with the baby Princess Louise (now Duchess of Fife) on her hack. " Of course. taking professions. the stage claims the largest sale 2" "Precisely. The public prefer photcgranhs of stage celebrities to any others. But the popularity of actresses' portraits fluctuates very mush indeed. There is one actress who must have been taken in quite two hundred different attitudes, and probably hers is the most popular just now. Mitgi Ellen Terry, I should say, ranks next, and Miss Julia Neilson a good third. The shelves in some photogrann shops must really be cram- med with unsaleable stuff, for it is not generally known that many actresses call at the shops themselves with their own photographs and beg the shopkeepers to expose them to view. I have known actors to do the same thing. Occasionally the dealer makes a charge for exhibiting a photograph; and this is only business, as it is a splendid thing for an actor or actress to have his or her portrait displayed in the shop-windows." "It's a daring and fearfully sacrilegious question to ask, but over how many years does the de- mand for an actress's photograo extend 2" " Until she's married. Married actresses' photographs, for the most part, area drug in the market. It is the single

ladies who swallow up all the trade." "Taking them o•enerally, what about the portraits of actresses who are rint paragons of beauts.--that is tO Say, if such exi.t ?" " They don't sell. It is benutv that brings in the shekels—not brains. Scientists' photos very seldom "And who buys these lovely pictures ?" "Men buy the portraits of actresses generally, while as regards actors it is the other way about. The nsost popular actors' photographs are those of Mr. Irving,Mr. Beerbohm Tree as Hamlet, and Mr. George Alexander, In the order named. Last year Mr. George Alexander's probably sold best ; but, as I've told you, the popu- larity of phttographs fluctuates a great deal," ,• Is there a brisk trade in the portraits of titled ladies?" "The demand is only a trifling one, but of all titled ladies the photograph of the late Duchess of Llostnr sold the best. As regards military nun, Lord Roberts's portrait is in greatest demand."

HALTON PARISH COUNCIL.

A meeting of this Council was held at Halton on Tues- day evening. The Rev. G. D. Wray occupied the chair and there were also present, Messrs. J. Stokes, W. McCulloch, R. Hughes, J. Ackerley, H. Reynolds, J. S. Jeacock, C. Birch, S. Barrow, J. Looker and A. Woodier (clerk). The Council took into consideration the con- tracts for repointing the walls around the Cemetery, and also for painting. It was decided that the contract of Mr. William Ashley, contractor, Halton, for the pointing of the boundary wall at £l2 75., ond the contract of Mr. George Barker, plumber and painter, for painting at £9 be accepted. It was reported that four interments had taken place during the past month.—The Chairman again introduced the subject of the charities, and said the Cannon could only deal with those which belonged to the, township of Halton alone. Most of the charities belonged to the ecclesiastical parishes of Halton, Norton Clifton and Stockharn, and therefore the Halton Council could not act independently of the three places which he had mentioned. There was only one charity—the St. Thomas' charity —which was exclusively for Halton, and that had I already been banded over to the Parish Council. In appointing trustees for the charities it would be neces- sary to have one from each of the townships concerned. —Mr. Hughes asked whether there was still a balance in hand of £B7 6s. 10d. on the Stockham charity as 1 shown on the statement printed.—The Chairman said £5O of that sum had been re-invested by the Charity Commissioners. The charity used to belong entirely to Stockham for the relief of widows attending the Church of England, but as there were no persons in need of it and the money kept accumulating, he suggested that the charity should be thrown open to the poor of Halton. The Commissioners fell in with the suggestion and it was accordingly done. Since then he had given away as much as he could. He thought there was about £lO in hand.—Mr. Hughes said he hoped the Chairman would excuse him for saying It, but he was the first parson in his (Mr. Hughes') experience that did not know what to do withlmoney. (Laughter.) The Chairman said if Mr. Hughes would hand him ever £lOO it should not be lost for want of spending. (Laughter.)—Mr. Hughes said he never came across a parson who could get enough. (Renewed laughter.)—Mr. Jeacock said it would be better to have the matter mentioned to the other Councils concerned so that they might get the matter settled.—Mr. Hughes proposed that a committee consisting of the Chairman, Mr.Reynolds,and Mr. Looker be appointed to confer with the Clifton, Norton and Stockham Councils.—Mr. Ackerley seconded the proposi- tion which was carried.—This concluded the business.

PRESENTATION TO DR. REGINALD SMITH, MB.

DISTRIBUTION OF AMBULANCE MEDALS AND

CERTIFICATES.

On Tuesday evening week the members of the Warring- ton Y.M.C.A.Ambulance Classassembled to receive their medals and certificates of merit Mr.S.B.Stringer occu- pied the chair, and after a few introductory remarks, Mr. H. Stewart, the general secretary of the Associa- tion, announced with much regret the absence, through a slight illness, of Mr. F. S. Buzza, the honorary secre- tary of the class. Apologies were also read from Messrs T. H. Hardy, ambulance brigade secretary, and H. T. Williams. A very short but effective musical programme was arranged with Mr. Padmore (organist, St. Ann's Church), presiding at the piano. Mr. W. Woods rendered the solos, "Anchored," and "The Village Blacksmith," in good style, after which the Chairman called upon Dr. Reginald Smith to present the medals and certificates to the successful candidates. Dr. Smith took the oppor- tunity of paying a warm tribute to Mr. F. S. Buzza, ' whose work as secretary of the class since its inception has been markPd with untiring zeal and thoroughness. The follewing gentlemen were then awarded third year bronze medals: F. S. Buzza, A. G. Gartland, H. T. Williams, Joseph Taylor, and J. B. Doncaster; certifi- cates: J. J. Armstrong, W. Clegg, J. Keenan, A Monn- field, R Newcomb, W. Richards. The Chairman briefly alluded to the value of a little practical knowledge of ambulance work, and stated that his own connection with the class a few years ago had given him some hints which had enabled him to render very helpful assistance to a person whose life was in danger after having been rescued from drowning.

The CHAIRMAN then expressed the hope that Dr. Reginald Smith would add to the well earned fame which his esteemed father and grand father had merited in the medical profession—(applause)—and trusted that, as the son and grandson of gentlemen who had rendered honourable service to the town he would follow in their footsteps and maintain the family reputation as a skil- ful medical practitioner in Warrington. He (the Chair- man) felt mulh honoured when invited to perform the pleasant duty of making the presentation which the members of the class had asked him to do. and on their behalf would ask Dr. Reginald Smith to accept the walking stick which had been selected as a very small token of their appreciation of his valuable services as honorary instructor. Dr. SMITH on rising to re- ply, was warmly received, and in a brief hut appreciative manner stated that he was thoroughly convinced, judeine- tw the valuable and handsomely engraved walking stick just presented to him, his ser- vices must have been valued accordingly. He had I great pleasure in nentVinz the present and ,ished the Y.M.O A. Ambulance Class much success. (Apnlause ) —Mr. Mills,the Treasurer of the Warrinoton Ambula nee Brigade spoke a few worls of encouritrement to thP memhers,especially emphasizing, the necessity of' keep;ng up their knowledge by joisins- in the regular practices , conducted by the Brigade.—Mr. Ireland then rendered s very pleasing solo for which he was encored. A few further remarks from the Chairman, and the usual votes of thanks brought the meeting to a close. The examination took place on April 4th and was con- ducted by Dr A.Chad wick, of Heaton Chapel, Stockport.

GLENALPINE'S OATH;

OR, THE MARK OF THE BLACK CROSS.

-- B Y BYLVANIIS COBB, JB.

This is a story of love and war, and is full of adventures in the Highlands of Scotland, on the sea, and in the jungles of India. It is intensely interesting from the first to the last.

Lionel Talford, and all his energies were bent upon Increasing the pile of gold and bank-notes which had already grown to a goodly size beneath hi dexterous manipulations. At first he had played with seeming honesty; but as he added more and more fuel to the liquid fire that heated his blood he became reckless any daring, and his winning- hands were marvellous to behold. _ _

Arthur Berwick had fallen once again under the dominion of his direst curse. For winning money he cared nothing. He played simply for the ex- citement of the thing, and played deeply only when his senses were overcome by liquor. His brain was of that exceedingly sensitive texture which, while strong and well-balanced in its nor- mal state, becomes a pool of fire under the influence of unhealthy stimulant. To a certain limit he could indulge without visible effect, but when once the temptation of the occasion had led him beyond that limit he was like a ship without a rudder. The only bound to his debauch after the line of safety had been passed was his ability to stand and swallow. There was one peculiarity in his temperament which rendered it exceedingly unsafe for him to venture upon excess in the use of liquor. His reason was wont to desert him long before his companions found occasion to mis- trust it. Plans and impressions fixed in his mind might go on developing themselves through a power of instinct which nothing short of sheer coma could overcome; but the mental work was not of his own sanity, and when the fit was passed and reason dawned again, the season of his debauch was to him an utter blank upon the events of which his memory had no hold. Arthur had taken his seat at the table with the impression that Talford intended to cheat if he could, and he meant to watch him. At length he began to lose rapidly and the lieutenant began to win. The two captains played carefully, as did Harold; but Arthur was in for the excitement, and he made up his hand at every deal. He drank deeply and watched the play with unsteady eyes. He was growing drunk very fast. At length he detected 'Pafford in the act of secreting the pant. Talford had held a "pain-flush," and had swept the board; and it was Arthur's next deal. When he gathered up the cards he observed that the lieutenant only gave him four, having retained the fifth.

"Where is the pam?" demanded Arthur, as he turned over the four cards and found the Jack of Clubs missing. "I guess it's in the pack somewhere," replied Talford, quietly. "No, sir; it's not in the pack. You've got it." Talford moved back his chair and the missing card dropped from his lap to the floor. He stooped and picked it up, and as he handed it back he said, laughingly: "Excuse me; it was an awkward blunder." "Awkward for you," retorted Arthur, "since it exposed your trickery." " Trickery ? " repeated Telford, flushing. "I said TRICKERY, sir." "Mr. Berwick, do you accuse me of having intended to cheat ?" "Not exactly of having intended the thing," replied Arthur, bluntly, "bet you have been cheat- ing all along." "Arthur Berwick, if you say that I have cheated you say that which is false." Capt. Lundie would have interfered at this point, but he was too late. Arthur's next words were already out: "And you, Lionel Talford, if you say I speak falsely you LIE ! " Talford's face changed from red to write, but he restrained himself. "I said that you spoke falsely if you said I had cheated."

"And I," cried Arthur, in the heat of his insane passion, his brain aflame with the fire of the cup, say that you are a cheat and a liar both ! You have cheated repeatedly end now you lie to hide "Bah ! you are a crazy fool. If you were sober you would not say so." Arthur was verging towards sobriety very fast, but there was much fire yet to be extinguished. • "Do you mean to say that I am drunk ? " he demanded, with deepest indignation. "Drunk as a fool " returned Talford, con- temptuously. " Don't be alarmed, gentlemen," said Harold, aside to the two captains; "there is no danger of Arthur's venturing into a personal conflict. He is not bold enough for that." Arthur heard this, as the speaker had meant he should hear it, and with no thought of conse- quences—with only passion in his soul—he sprang over the table and caught Talford by the throat. The lieutenant was as a child in the grasp of the stalwart, muscular youth, and, with a vain effort to maintain himself, he was borne back against one of the pillars. " You cheated ! you lied ! and now you call me a fool !" howled Arthur. And as the culminating act of his madness he struck Talford in the face. He did not strike him with his fist, but with his open palm upon the cheek. With this act the unfortunate young man seemed to have expended his insane wrath, for he quickly started back, as though ashamed of what he had done. "In Heaven's name, gentlemen," cried Harold, with well-affected horror, "let us stop this work where it is ! We must stop it for the present. Captain Lundie—Captain Bashaw—will you care for Talford? I will look to the other." Talford, smarting under the blow he had received, as well as under the opprobrious epithets which had been cast upon him, had no disposition to appeal to brute force for redress, nor had he a thought of settling the matter in any way upon the spot; so he offered no resistance when his friends took him by the arms. He had received the assurance from Harold, in a whisper, that he should have ample opportunity for satisfaction, and he was content to be led from the room. "My dear brother," said Harold, when they were left alone, "I am sorry for this. But you had better retire now." Arthur, who had been leaning against a pillar, looked up with a bewildered stare, and pressed his hand upon his brow. "Harold, is this you ?"

" Yea." "Where is—is —Talford ?" "He is gone." "Did I strike him ?" "Yes, Arthur, you struck him. But let us not talk of it now. We will think it over in the mcrning." "Did he strike me ?"

"My dear boy, I cannot tell you now what he did. Let us say no more about it to night. You are not in a mood to think freely, nor to under- stand clearly. Wouldn't a drop of brandy do you good?" "Yes, yes, Harold let me have some brandy. I feel badly." He drank half a tumbler of brandy as he would have drank so much water, and then turned away. Lie took a few steps, and then stopped. "Look you, old fellow, you'll tell me about this in the morning ?" "Yes."

"All right. The brandy had for the moment revived hie crushed spirits, but he stopped for no further remark. He made his way, with uncertain steps, to his own chamber, where, ere long, his senses were locked in lethargic slumber. Lady Birchmear had not yet retired. Her son found her awaiting his report. " Your face tells me," she said, as soon as he had closed the door of her boudoir, and had turned towards her, "that you have gained a point in the game." " You shall hear.

They sat down, and Harold related the incidents which had transpired in the old apartment of the donjon. Her ladyship listened attentively and with deep interest. " You mean," she said, after a little reflection. "that a duel shall grow out from this " "Something like a duel my mother." "And do you mean that Arthur shall be shot ? " Harold did not reply. He was engaged with his own thoughts. "Will they not fight?" asked the lady. 1I hope so." "You do not think Arthur would shrink—"

_ Pshaw ! He would shrink from nothing. If there comes a hitch, it mtrt be on the other side. Talford's companions may try to prevent his fight- ing with a member of the family whose hospitali- ties they have accepted. But I hope to bring it about, nevertheless. I think you may set it down that there will be a meeting to-morrow. And now, my mother, I want you to help me to anticipate the result. You have keys to Sir John's private cabinet ?" 4 4 yes..

"Will you get me two thousand pounds there- from ?"

"Two thousand pounds?" "It will find its way to Arthur's pocket book." Lady Birchmear understood perfectly well. She arose, and took a candle and left the room, and was gone some time. When she returned she was con- siderably agitated. and sank into a seat like one

CaArTER rzz.

THE CARD-TABLE. The day of the party arrived and passed. The weather was delightful, the attendance was large, and - the whole thing was voted unanimously a perfect success. My Lady Birchmear was atten- tive to the comfort of her guests, but she did not once forget her husband. When Lord Oxnard asked her to dance with him, she declared that she would dance with no one until she had first danced with her husband; and that, furthermore, she would only dance when he danced. So Lord Oxnard joined my lady in beseiging the baronet, and at length he led his wife out for the dance. What a clapping and a cheering there was when Sir John went tripping over the velvety turf. His wife whispered to the blooming matrons and maidens of the party to help her in her work, and they lent their help to a purpose. My lady kept her promise not to dance save when her husband danced, and yet she danced very often. But after dinner Sir John was quiet. Major Thorpe was with him, and they talked of the old scenes in India over their wine. And other choice spirits were there—peers and gentlemen —who joined heartily in the enjoyment of the occasion. By ten o'clock most of the guests had departed, only those remaining who were to spend the night. Sir John had bade Lord and Lady Oxnard adieu—had seen the last carriage depart—and had then joined Major Thorpe over a fresh bottle of wine. Meantime Harold Berwick had improvised an entertainment of his own in one of the apartments of the old donjon' where he had assembled Arthur and three officers from Sandsport. Of these officers the youngest first deserves our notice. He was Lieutenant Lionel Talford, a young man of four- and-twenty, slight but strongly built, with a handsome face, and a keen grey eye. He was a daring, reckless fellow, thrown early into corrupt- ing associations, and now, in his early manhood, acknowledged as the most accomplished gamester of his mess. Naturally Talford had a warm and impulsive heart, but those impulses had been so long bent in a wrong direction that the very instincts of his nature, which might have made him a good companion, were now only developed In danger and evil to those who associated with him. And this man Harold had brought to Birchmear for a purpose. The other officers were Captain Bashaw and Captain Lundie, two men who had the reputation of being brave soldiers, but who were far more fond of wine and cards than of the book of tactics. It will be understood that the party had arisen from the dinner-table well filled with wine, so that they were ripe for sport when they entered the old donjon. The apartment into which they had been ushered was a large, vaulted room, the roof supported by atone pillars, and the only windows being such narrow loopholes as were wont to be made In the walls of embattled towers. Upon the walls of the apartment, and upon the pillars, were hung arms, offensive and detensive, of all kinds, and of many different periods; and as the great hanging lamps had been newly filled and trimmed, casting from their globes of coloured glass a rich glare upon the quaintly burdened walls, the place bore an aspect peculiarly suited to the tastes and whims of the guests. While the two captains, guided by Arthur, went into ecstasies over the grand old arms and armour of bygone times, Harold led Lionel Talford apart for a private conference. "Hark you," said Talford, whom large quantities of wine had rendered particularly self-important, but who was yet willing to serve a friend. " I'll do anything in reason. To tell you the truth, Berwick, I like the fellow. He 's got a grand, big heart inside his jacket." "And yet, Talford, he is one of the most self- sufficient and overweening men you ever met." " Egad 1 I haven't seen it." "Because he has only shown you the fair side. But it is only for sport. I simply wish to give him a lesson. We won't harm him. But you shall see his temper before you are through with him." "All right, old boy. lam your guest, and will hold myself under orders. If, as you say, the lad is self-sufficient, we must take him down a peg; and if he is inclined to show his temper over a social game of cards, we 'll try what stuff he has at the bottom of it. We 'll have some sport out of It, if nothing more." "Only sport, Talford ; though I want the lesson to be a severe one—such an one :Ls he will remember. And, mark you: If he is going into the army it will do him good." " Zouncis !" muttered Talford to himself, aftet Harold had turned away. "I don't half like this. Berwicu wants to fasten a quarrel upon the young fellow, and he would throw me into the breach. I'd rather it wer himself I was to engage. Con- found him! I think he 's a coward. But he is treating me like a prince, and I must not fail him." ' - .

Meantime Harold sought an opportunity to draw Arthur aside. During the day he had been very kind and attentive to his youthful step-brother; so much so that the latter had really imagined that for once the heart of his rival had become softened by the warmth of sociality. Never disposed to find evil where he could possibly find good, Arthur was so far from possessing a suspicious nature that he preferred not to suspect, particularly where suspicions could only bring clouds and unrest. On the present occasion he fully believed that Harold was aiming to pass the time away as pleasantly as possible, and he meant to do his share towards the same end. The wine he had already drunk had so warmed and opened his heart that he was ready for anything that might afford gleeful entertainment a heart warmed and opened, but with its Impulses of reason stolen away; warmed to a degree that gave it up to the molding influences of whatever passion might besiege it, and open to the incoming of friend and foe alike. When wine warms and opens a heart like unto that heart, it first overcomes and sets aside the sober sentinel which stands guard at the portals of sense and reflection, and which holds the citadel of manhood against evil. "Arthur," whispered the plotter, "before we sit down to our cards I want to give you a word of caution. Lieutenant Talford is, as you can see, a glorious fellow in true sport, but he has the reputation of being entirely unscrupulous at cards. If we are to call things by their right names, I should say that he is inclined to cheat when he can get an opportunity. So be careful how you play against him." "I'll look out for him, never fear," returned Arthur, confidently. "But don't anger him," whispered Harold. He is dangerous. He might do you harm.' Arthur straightened up with indignation. "Do ME harm !" he said. "By the Lordllarry he had better try it." "Keep on the safe side, Arthur. Watch him narrowly, and if you find him cheating, give him a gentle hint. You had better not anger him, for he makes his boast that no man can do that with impunity. And one thing more," Harold added, as the youth was about to speak impulsively, "you must remember that Talford is a soldier who has seen service, and who knows nothing of fear. You would be no match for him." Arthur was too deeply indignant to hear more. With an oath he whirled upon his heel, and strode away to where the two captains were standing by one of the pillars examining a full suit of armour which a noble knight of Birchmear had worn at Agincourt. Harold smiled and shook his head. He saw that his game was working. All that remained for him to do was to ply the liquor and let things take their course. A servant brought in rum and brandy and lemons, and Harold with his own hands brewed a strong punch. Arthur drank with Capt. Bashaw, and then with Capt. Lundie. Then Talford claimed the privilege of touching his glass. And before the bout was finished Harold demanded to drink with his brother. A second bowl of the strong decoction was brewed, and set in the middle of the card-table, after which the party sat down to their game. As there were five players the game selected was fipam-Zoo," with "five shillings for deal," and " ten shillings for loo." The game went on with much sport and laugh- ter, while the punch-bowl was emptied and re- plenished more than once. There were two of the party, however, who did not join so heartily in the laugh and 5 that companions. The demon of the ttble had seized noon

may be sure you will have. (Applause.) But in a very nub larger ineseure, it rests with you. (Hear, hear.) 'peaking is all very well in its way, but that will not sein this elecetton nor any other. You must go out and eork, not only yourselves, but you must get others to ssist you., and to judge from the number I flee hereeand he enthusiasm with which you have received me, I have very tittle doubt as to what the result will be. (Loud pplause.) I have one word to say ; I would ask you all 0 let this contest be fought in such a manner, that whatever the issue may be, our opponents will not be able 'to say we have done anything which is not above board, and we have not extended the courtesy which is due from one party to another. (Applause.) Gentle- men, I know my opponent is, what I hope lam my- . sell—a gentleman first, and a politician afterwards (loud applause.) I wish to speak to you for a few moments on why we are here tonight. As you are aware, the Govern- ment were defeated by a majority of seven on a paltry issue, after Mr. Campbell Bannerman—now Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman—had announced to the House of Commons that H.R.H. the Duke of Cambridge would renounce the commmandership of the British army—after I say, announcing that no longer will we hare to pay the large stipend which he has enjoyed so long,but that we shall have at the head of the ermy a man of experience—practical experience—and knowledge of affairs which would command respect— after announcing this, the Government was defeated— defeated because Sir H. Campbell Bannerman refused to make public the secrets of the army which could only benefit the enemies of our country. (Shame.) Gentle- men, the War minister was one of the most popular, if not the most popular minister of modern times,— (epplause)—and in consequence of that defeat, you havenow a Government, which (venture to say is (" a body of traitore,")anything but in accordance with your views. (Hear, hear.) Gentlemen, what is the Govern- ment .? ("Turn coats," and laughter.) They say it is a Liberal 'Unionist Government. It is nothing of the kind. It it a coalition Government, of which the late Lord Beaconsfield said" England does not love coali- tions." To those who differ from me, I would ask what do you expect to get from a coalition Government ? You will get nothing. They will promise,but they will not perform. (Hear, hear.) And this Government, which comprises Lord Salisbury and Mr. Chamberlain, (hisses and cries of" Judas") will not last very long. (Applause.) I have read in a good old book, a book which 'I think will last longer than any other—that the lion will lie down with the lamb. There may be truth in that, but I don't believe it is possible for two lions to keep the same quarters for any length of time. (Laughter and applauee.) I would like to read to you what Lord Tweedmouth says with regard to the defeat of the late Government. He was speaking at Torquay on Saturday last. He says : " They had a sad example in himself of the wrongs the House of Lords could do to an individual. He found himself 'under considerable disabilities now as a peer e, disabilities which he shared with three classes—criminals, lunatics, and worse. Like them, he was incapable of haying a voice in the election of M.Pee, and prevented from sitting in the House of Commons. Ho had a still further disadvantage of not being allowed to open his mouth during an election. The late Government was defeated, but not on a great ques- tion of policy. They were done to death in the dark by the assassin's stab in the back. At a moment when the War Minister had announced a great administrative change which would alter the whole administration of 'the Army, on a secondary point trumped up for the oc- casion, the Government was defeated. It had been a common taunt of the Tory party that they clung to the sweets of office. They had not been able to extract from Lord Salisbury his policy (cries of" Dissolution.") That was an ominous word that suggested corruption. It savoured of the churchyard, and he ventured to say that it portended the early funeral of those who adopted it as their cry." Since the last election the greatest blow the Liberal party has ever received is not only the re- tirement from the leadership of the party, but the re- tirement permanently from the deliberations o, the Im- perial Parliament, of the Right Hon. William Ewart Gladstone. (Loud applause.) Mr. Morley said the other evening, that that was not the only disappearance. There was a further disappearance, and gentlemen, I am glad to say we can afford this one, although we can ill afford the other. I refer to the disappearance of what was called the 'Unionist party. (Laughter and applause.) That has gone, and we shall not again, I hope, hear, as we have now heard for many years, of the sacrifices which that party—l won't call it a parteeit is not a perty —we have heard the last I hope of what that remnant has professed it has lost and suffered so much. (Ap- plause.) They have amalgamated with the Tories, and we wish them joy. We don't want them. We waist no bastard politicians. There is only room for two reirties in this state, the Liberals and the Tories. (Applause.) I am now going to say a few words about what is called Home Rule for Ireland. (Applause.) As I have told you in my election address, I am in favour of a Bill for the better government of Ireland, but one which at the same time secures the interests of the Imperial Parliament. (Applause.) lam in favour of the Bill which Mr. Gladstone introduced at the opening of the last Parlia- ment, in every shape and form. (Applause.) During the last three years,lreland has never been so contented, never so happy, not if Yeti look back during the whole of the past century. (Applause.) And to what do you attribute it? I attribute it to two canoes s first and foremost to the fact that the Irish people are looking for- ward to this measure of Home Rule. Their aspirations and their whole thoughts are centred in this one great boon. The other cause is the administration of Mr. Morley during his secretaryship of Ireland. (Applause.) I have come down here to- night to support my friend Mr. Scott, for two or three reasons. In the first place because I hope in Warring- ton, at all events lam not a stranger. During the last contest, aye, and in many contests before—when you returned my old friend, Mr. McMinnies—(loud ap. plause)—l have spoken on Warrington platforms to Warrington electors, and amongst those who were on those platforms4.l reckon many personal friends who have regarded me I think to a certain extent as one of the fathers of tbe, Liberal party in Lancashire. The 1 second reason for my coining here to-night is that I am to some extent responsible for Mr. Scott appearing be- fore you as a candidate. When your representatives came to see me, I told them that it would be a burning shame if they did not fight the seat, and I said if you cannot get another man I will come and fight it myself —(laughter and applause)—and I should have liked to have gone straight for my friend over there (pointing to a demonstrative gentleman in the audience). I have come here to-night to support Mr. Scott, and after I leave here, I have to go to Salford to speak there. The issue has been put before you by Mr. Scott in plain simple word, and I call upon you to show your pluck and stand by him in the fight. (Applause.) The ques- tion is, Shall we freeborn Englishmen govern ourselves? (Yes ) Shall we be governed by a miserable clique of coroneted nobodies? (Na, no.. I care nothing for that amalgamated company that forms our Government at the present day. They call themselves, God knows what. (Laughter.) Tories I know, Liberals I know, but what on earth are Tory Liberal Unionists. (Loud laughter.) The Liberal Democrat is one who believes in the right of the people to rule themselves, for them- selves, by themselves. (Applause.) The Tory party is the party of privilege, and is the supporter of privilege, but what this amalgamation is God knows, but I don't. (Laughter.) But it matters very little. Hybrids pro- duce nothing. That is a fact in natural history— (laughter)—and It was Lord Beaconsfield himself who, in one of his early novels, described the party of which he was afterwards leader—the great Conservative party —as the mule of politics which engenders nothing. That is what is done in this amalgamation. But what I don't agree with is that hereditary irresponsible barrier which stands across our path—the path of us free men, and shall the government of this country be under the control of that House where Lord Salisbury says a thing shall be done, or shall not be done. (Applause) If you are Englishmen with self respect in your hearts, if you are Englishmen and believe in the future of this great country,sweep it away. (Ap- plause.) I call upon you to fight the battle, but if you are craven curs, who are willing to put your necks down to he trodden noon by Lord Salisbury, a man who with all his intellectual power has not the slightest con- ception of the needs of his fellow countrymen, a man entirely secluded in his aristocratic ideas, and regarding his fellow countrymen as mere pawns on the chess board for him to move at his will, then you deserve what you get. (Applause.) I feel strongly on this question, be- cause I feel that our manhood is in question. (Hear, hear.) It is not merely because yesterday, or the day before, or last year, or the year before,that they rejected this or that measure—that they rejected the claim of our Irish fellow countrymen, or the Employers' Liability Bill—that I oppose the House of Lords. It is because We are free men, and have the right to govern ourselves. (Applause.)lf you like to go back to the old days,you will see you were governed by an absolute monarch. There was something in that, because the people as a whole were represented in the crown. But here we have the fortuitous atoms of society (laughter) ; men who come there they know not how. I care not how they go away from it. (Laughter.) They are there in the interests of privilege, and I say that all free born Englishmen shall exercise their rights of self government in spite of them. (Applause.) My work at election times is not so much speaking I have enough of that—it is mainly trying to arrange the affairs of the party so as to win elections. I have been two or three times in War- rington. There is never any lack of pluck, of the hard work or enthusiasm. There are men upon the platform like Mr, tierpoint, or Mr. Harry Roberts with whom I have been associated in many contests, not only in War- rington, but elsewhere. There is no lack of wise and competent administrators. But there is a lack not here alone, but in many constituencies, of that personal en- thusiasm which induces every man, aye, and every woman, to fed the greatness and the righteousness of the contest, and who put all their energies into the fight. It is not enough that we should record our vote. It is not enough that we should simply have a canvass book. It is the workers who year by year, in season and out of season, at home, in the workshop, are endeavouring to persuade others to follow in the right way. Persuade, I say, not force, because for my part, I have infinite res- pect for the good old Tory, for we then know whom we are fighting. But we are fighting this amalgama- tion, I have spoken of, just now. This is of a moat deceptive and wily character. The programme which has been put forward on their behalf is intended to deceive the electorate. When Lord Salisbury spoke in the House of Lords on Saturday, and mentioned the chief items in that programme he finished every owl by saying "this is not a panacea." He wanted to leave himself a loophole, through which to escape if his party got the upper hand. If they have a narrow majority at the next election, Joe Chamberlain will drive the coach—(laughter)—,and he will upset it. (Renewed laughter.) I invite you all to come to the funeral. (Laughter)—My friend there especially (again pointing to the demonstrative gentleman). But if the Tories get a large majority, there will be ructions. (Laughter.) There will be a shine in the tents of Shem. The young Tory bloods already are protesting that the Liberal Unionists are having the lion's share of the offices. There will be such a happy family,and we shall so enjoy it. I invite you to look at that also. (Laugh- ter.) But don't let us allow them to get there. (Loud applause.) There is no reason why they should. (Hear, hear.) An eminent and most respectable old Tory statesman, Sir Stafford Northcote, a man for whom I had an infinite respect,said not many months before his death, that the lower down you went in the strata of ignorance, the stronger you find the support for the Tory party. Well now, I hays held for a long time that Toryism is ignorance. (Applause.) But it is honest. But this hybrid business, Liberal Unionism, is dis- honesty. (Applause.) If you read the speeches of Joseph Chamberlain, he speaks of many measures we have been supporting, as if he wanted them to pass,but he could not support them. But as the old song says "It's all very well to disisenable your love, but why did you kick me downstairs? '(Applause.)A marvellous thing has happened in the House of Lords with respect to the Factory Act. I don't know how many pages,how many clauses it contains,but it is one of the longest bills before the House last session, and they passed it in one day. (Laughter.) And yet they say they are to check hasty legislation by the representatives of the people. (Laugh- ter.) They are funny dogs. (Reoewed laughter.) But we will have a change in the play. Let them sleep there, if they like. I (Laughter.) It will be a grand sight to see the peers sleeping in their night-shirts and coronets. (Loud laugh- ter.) We will keep them, they are really picturesque. (Renewed laughter.) They can do no harm as long as we have the legislative power. (Applause.) We want to deatrov all their legislative powers. (Hear, hear.) Lord Salisbury has said that if we took away the legisla- tive power from the House of Lords, you will give legis- lative autocracy to the House of Commons. Legislative autocracy is rather a long phrase. but it simply means the power to govern yourselves. I am not afraid of that. (Applause.) We will chance it at any rate. (Laughter and Applause.) Lord Salisbury says that the Tories support property and liberty. You see he takes good care to put property first. (Hear, hear.) He said that the Liberals were interfering with liberty. Well, look at the Local Veto Bill. This Bill simply gives you the power to decide for yourselves whether you will or will not have any publichouses. I fancy Lord Salis- bury thinks more about property than liberty in this case. (Applause.) He also says that you cannot say that any parliament has been elected for the passing of any particular Bile and if the Liberals are returned by a large majority, he will say that some were returned for Local Veto, none for Home Rule, and some for disestab- lishment, he will then say that the House of Lords has a perfect right to reject, Bills. (Loud applause.) The Rev. W. H. DRUMMOND met with a very cordial reception on rising to second the resolution. He said : Mr. Chairman, I have very great pleasure in seconding the resolution, that we heartily adopt Mr. Scott as the Liberal candidate for Warrington. I have seen some people shake their heads in a sorry fashion during the past week over the idea of having a contest, and I have heard some people murmur dark prophecies about the unwisdom of the Liberal party at this particular junc- ture trying to join issue in this constituency, but I think that the magnificent and enthusiastic meeting we'have to-night is quite sufficient confirmation of the wisdom of those of us who did what we could to urge on the Liberal party that we should contest this election. (Hear, hear.) I will not keep you more than a moment or two to say very briefly why I am on this platform, and why with all the sympathy both of my intellect and my heart I support the Liberal cause. (Hear, hear.) I think first of all at the present time the reason is this— that I have Irish blood in my veins. (Applause.) I am an Irishman born in Lancashire. (Laughter and applause.) I think it is a tolerably grrA amalgam for modern Radicalism. (Applause.) But I do feel that we have arrived at a time of very grave crisis in regard to the Irish question. Our friends of the other side are trying to persuade themselves that the Irish question has ceased to exist in practical politics. ("No.") Mr. Pierpoint in his address tells us that you have only got to return the Unionists to power to give Home Rule its death blow. (" Nonsense.") Mr.lherpoint can have very little political foresight. (Rear, hear.) I take it that the wonderful tranquility of Ireland at the present time is due almost entirely to the fact that Irish- men have learned to trust the great English democracy. (Hear, hear.) If anything happens to cause Irishmen to find out that their confidence in Eng- land has been misplaced, if instead of the wise, patient and devoted administration of Mr. Morley in Ireland— (loud aplauser—we have the administration of Mr. Ger- ald Balfour, who, so far as we know, has no very pro- nounced sympathy with Irish aspirations, than I very much fear a wave of deep and strong resentment will be felt throughout the length and breadtts of Ireland.(Hear, hear.) If we want to wipe out, so far as we can that disgraceful page of English history, if we want to prevent any recurrence of these methods of dealing withl reland, methods which should bring a blush to the cheeks of every man who values the name of free man, then I say we must , cast a solid vote on the side of the party which has a liv- ing sympathy with Ireland, (Loud and continued ap- plause.) Secondly, I am a supporter of the Liberal party on this occasion, because the Liberal party has identi- fied itself with the forward movement of English democracy. (Apnlause ) I believe very strongly and with a growing streagth not only from the sympathy of my heart, but I think I may also say from some study of the question and for some intellectual reasons which to me, at any rate, are sound, I believe strongly in democracy, and if you look at the present political situation you can have no 'shadow of a doubt which party of politicians are most largely animated with sympathy for popular demands. (Hear, hear, and applause.) You will find that our opponents are making a great deal of the fact that they say the Liberal Government has done very little. It has been bothering itself about Home Rule in Ireland, about Welsh Disestablishment and other things,but it has done nothing for the great mass of the working people in our country. Now, I assert unhesitatingly, that no state- ment more entirely false to the facts could possibly be made. (Loud applause.) The things which sometimes tell in elections are the big Bills which are passed through the two houses of legislature, but after all a great deal of the happiness and the progress of the people depends upon wise and good administration, and if I may single one department of the late Government's work, I should say that in the whole course of English history the Home Office has not been managed in a way so conducive, to the progress and well-being of the people as has been the case under the direction of Mr. Asquith. (Loud applause.) Let me just venture to put before you one very brief passage in a speech which Mr. Asquith delivered Showing the benefit which the action of the Home Office has conferred upon the great working masses. " At this moment," said Mr. Asquith, "Tam thankful to say, in the Potteries of Staffordshire, in the chemical an i lead works of this and other counties, in the flax and linen manufactories of Belfast, in the open quarries in all parts of England and Wales, in the sweaters' dens in the East End of London—(cheers)—in the overcrowded and insanitary workshops!of the West—in all those CASPS life and limta are more secure, poisonous processes have been rendered comparatively innocuous, health is protected, sanitation is ensured, illegal overtime has been stopped, and the safeguards which the law has intended to inter- pose between the cupidity and cruelty of the worst class of employers and those who, from age or sex, from poverty or ignorance, are too weak to defend themselves —those safeguards have become a living and working reality." (Loud applause.) I believe that in these elo- quent words. Mr. Asquith has accurately and within the strict limits of truth summed uo the benefits which the administratlon of the Liberal Government has conferred upon those who live and labour, the great masses of the people, and I say unheoitatingly, that it is from the Liberal Government that you have to look for a con- tinuance of similar administration and for those laws wnich are necessary to correct the great injustices which still exist and to continue the great movernent of up- ward democratic progress. ponents are putting themselves forward as the friends of social reform. (Laughter.) They tell us that we Liberals are simply engaged in dismembering and disintegrating empires and churches, while they are Yearning with all the love of their hearts to get into office that they may confer great and lasting benefits upon you the working people of the country. ROBERTS said : It is not my intention to address the meeting at any length to-night. There are, however, one or two things I would like to say. There are gentlemen in this room who, I daresay, occasionally read a newspaper published in Warrington, called the "Observer." (Laughter.) I have had a great deal to do with the keeping up of that newspaper. (Laughter.) It has not had any money out of me, but I have had some out of it—(renewed laughter)—, and I think the proba- bilities are that if it goes on as it is doing, it will not be my fault if I don't get some more. (Laughter.) What- ever you see in the " Observer " newspaper you accept it as gospel truth. I always do. (Laughter.) There was a statement in it last week to the effect that my poor insignificant self was mainly responsible for forcing a candidate upon the Liberal party during this election. I am not at all sorry if I am—(applause)—but I am very glad to tell you that lam not solely responsible. lam one of those who believe that it is the duty of all sup- porting the Liberal creed not merely to say so, but to fight for it whenever there is an opportunity. (Ap- plause.) This is not merely a local battle. It is a na- tional battle in which we are engaged. (Applause.) Although it may seem to some timid people to be an utter impossibility to win this , borough—which I don't admit for one mo- , ment—l think it is practicable and quite possible—(ap- plause)—it is none the less our duty to take our part in ' this great battle which is now taking place. We have I seen surprises before to-day. (Applause.) Warrington , has not always sent a Tory to Parliament, and it is not , going to continue always to send a Tory to Parliament. (Applause.) The majority by which we were defeated , at the last election was not a very big one. It only means the turning over of less than 300, and there you are. (Applause.) What are you going to do? ("Win.") What lam coming to is this. We cannot possibly win , this election from the platform. But we can win it from door to door. What we want is that you give in your names to-night, either to our friends at the doors, or leave them across at the Liberal Club,and that you take your share in this fight. I don't think some of our friends have the remotest idea of the amount of work and anxiety. and worry there is in connection with a contested election. But many helpers make easy work, (Hear, hear.) This is not a battle of those on the plat- form, so much to-night as the battle of the workingmen. We are anxious to fight. If you don't take the opportunity you now have before you, it is quite possible it won't occur again for many years. Mr. Pierpeint says in his address that if —and it is a very big "if" too—if the Conservative and Unionist party is returned to power, it will be the end of Home Rule. Of course, as I said just now, it is a very big "if." I don't believe for one moment, that the Unionist party is going to came back into power. (Applause.) I believe it will be found as the result of the elections that the Liberal party is in the ascendant. (Applause.) The Liberal party never has been defeated yet except by its own apathy, and by dissensions within its own ranks. (Hear, hear.) I believe also that we should not push forward too much our own particular ideas but go in solidly for the general principles common to the belief of all true Liberals. A Conservative auth- ority has said on one occasion that he did not care, and it did not matter where the man came from. If he were merely a stick, if he was a Tory, he would vote for him. (Laughter.) Without going exactly to the same length, I am prepared individually to say this, whoever the Liberal candidate is, no Twitter what his opinions may be on certain matters not germane to the general questions, I am going to vote for that Liberal candidate. (Applause.) Well, you all have to fight if you wish to have this matter brought to a successful issue. It can be done on Monday next, which I expect is the election day. (Applause.) We can work as well as the other side with a good deal less accessories than they. (Laughter.) We can do a great deal more work in a great deal less time than the other side. In the mean- time let all who at present are not on the ward commit- tees kindly let their names be known,and let them begin working for the candidate to-night. (Applause.) The purpose for which I rose to-night WAS to propose a vote of thanks to the Chairman, which I do without comment. (Applause.) Mr. PIERPOINT saidrthat he would take the resolution las seconded and carried. He would emphasise the words which Mr. Roberts had said. This election could not be won by talking from the platform so much as it could be by work from door to door by his friends in front of him. Mr. Scott and others would do all they could to help them. If they would read Mr. Scott's address, they would find that he was fighting for them,and they would show very great want of judgment and base ingratitude if they did not put their best energies into tho work and return him at the head of the poll. (Loud applause.) The meeting which had been roost enthusiastic throughout, then terminated, and the Liberal club was soon filled with willing workers who took out canvass books, and in a very short time, the whole town will be canvassed.

At the close of the meeting, Councillor Roberts announced that on Friday night a great meetina would be held at Ohmy's circus, and it is to b.N hoped that this large structure will be packed when the time for commencing the meeting arrives.
