ENTHUSIASTIC MEETING IN BRICK- STREET SCHOOLROOM.

MR. PIERPOINT'S ATTENDANCES.-REMARKABLE FIGURES. THE QUESTION OF THE CATHOLIC VOTE.

Brick-st. schoolroom was crowded to overflowing on Wednesday evening on the ()erasion of the meeting in support of the candidature of Mr. Scott. The chairman was Mr. J. H. Fleming, J.P., and he was accompanied on the platform by Mr. Scott, the candidate, Councillor Henry Roberts, J.P.. Messrs. J. Gandy, T. Mather, W. Furness,F.Dixon,T. B.Biernan,and Farnsworth (National Reform Union.) 1 The Cram/den said they were met there that night I, I hear an address from their candidate. He (Mr. Flem- ing) had not an opportunity of hearing him speak on Monday. but from what h. had read of the speech, he could say that Mr. Scott's views were quite in accord- ance with his desires, and he was sure they could not have a better candidate. Some might say he was young —(A Voice: That is the beauty of him)—but being young he would live to fight arother day. (Applause.) He had views which they all desired to see carried out. H. thought they all agreed with Home Rule for Ireland. (Cheers.) The bill passed the House of Commons, hut was thrown out by the House of Lords. His opinion was that if that House could not be mended it must be ended. (Cheers.) There were other important measures which the House of Lords had smothered, and it was now for the electors to express their opinion on the matter. (Applause.) Mr. PERCIVAL B. ScoTT who met with a most enthusias- tic reception said he had to thank them for the kind apd hearty reseption they had accorded him that night. They knew, of course, the position in which he stood, and it was not necessary for him to thank them on so large a scale as on Monday evening. They were told by their political friends—their opponents—that be was a stranger in a strange land. We'l, judging by their recep- tion and the kindness extended to him. he could say that he felt very much at home. (Hear, hear.) He had every intention of making it his political home—(hear, hear)— and what was more he hoped in the near future of mak- ing it is permanent home. (Hear, hear, and cheers). He did not know whether they had read the newspapers that day but if they had they would have noticed that Mr. Pierpoint was speaking the previous evening. His words to them that night would be somewhat in answer to his opponents' remarks. (Ch-ers.) Mr. Pierpoin` told them that in 1892 they (the electors) sentenced him to three veers hard labour. It was a serious thing—(laughter)— but he made the astounding statement that he wished for a further period of it.—(A Voice : Well, he won't get it). (Laughter). Well, h. (Mr. Scott) did not dispute that fact for a moment so far as he was physically con- cerned, but when he lonk.d back at what Mr. Pierpnint had done during the past three years, and how he had carried out what he prom,sed them if they would return him to Parliament, he (Mr. Scott) must advise them not to send him again. (Rear, hear). He had very good reasons for saying " dont ". (Hear, hear). For on tak- in notice of the returns which they had of ascertaining how their members carried out the wishes of the electors .1--and it was the only means, but it was a correct one of finding out what those gentlemen did for them—he could Ray that Mr. Pierpoint had not carried out what he promised them in 1892. (Hear, hear.) They had statistics of the divisions—important divisions— which had taken place in the House of Commons during the past three years. Be purposed reading them. In 1892 there was only one division of any great moment. Now Mr. Pierpoint attended that. In 1893 there were no less than 450 division and out of that number Mr. Pierpoint only attended 210—(shame)-210 out of 450. They now came to the year 1894. In that year there were 246 divisions—momentous divisions—and out of

.^

iC. 11

, those Mr. Pierpoint,their late member, only attended 52. (" Oh" and "Shame.") In the year 1895 up to the 22nd I of June, there were 138 divisions and Mr. Pierpoint only seemed pleased to grace the House of Commons with 48 appearances. Well that made a total during the past three years of no less than 835 divisions in the House of Commons at which the late member for Warrington only attended 311. If that was representationg that was the hard labour he referred to, he would say now, and he wished them to be very clear on that point, to take the test to heart, aad instead of giving Mr. Pierpoint six more years on the treadmill, send him to the Infirmary. (Laughter.) Ho now came to the late Government's work. Mr. Pierpoint stated that they hae done exceed- ingly little. There were differences of opinion as to what some people called little, and what others called much. For himself, he liked little, but good. (Hear, hear.) And when he looked back on what the Liberal Govern- ment professed they would do before the election of 1892, if they sent them to Westminster, and he found the number of bills which they had passed through the House of Commons, and which had been mutilated by the irres- ponsible body of peers, he was of the opinion they had done what they promised and it was not their fault the bills were not the statute book. (Cheers.) It would be a large order to carry in his mind the bills which the Liberal Government had carried through the Commons, and which the House of Lords had taken care should not become law. He would mem- time just a few Bills which they had to thank the House of Lords that they had not got ; for they had nobody else to blame but the House of Lords that the measures were not now at their disposal. (Hear, hear.) The Lords rejected the Home Rule Bill. On Monday night he dealt with that act largely. To-night he had not the time. But be wanted to point out that it was said that the House of Lords was a body of exemplary minds, of exemplary ideas, and that they were there for the pur- pose of preventing Bills becoming law when they had been too hurriedly passed by the House of Commons. (A voice : When it is against themselves). He (Mr. Scott) maintained that that argument only applied when the Liberal Government was in power. (Cheers.) The Home Rule Bill was before the House of Commans for 82 nights and the House of Lords disposed of that measure in less than two days. C' Shame.") The House of Lords did not sit for more than half-an-hour to an hour a time, but in the House of Commons the twelve o'clock rule was done away with and there were all night sittings. Then again what did the House of Lords do the previous week. They had the Factories and Workshops Act, a Bill which he ventured to say, had the Liberal Government been in power six months longer, would not have been on the statute book to-day. The Unionists dared not go to the country without passing that Bill. They passed it in less than one day. That was the hurried legislation which the House of Lords were there principally to prevent. That was what they, the electors, must remember, and that, of the Bills of moment passed by the late Government only one or two ' were graced with their Lordships' sanction. They killed the Employers' Liability Bill. They mutilated it so that the Government rightly and propeily refused to accept I the amendment which they inserted in the Bill after it I left the House of Commons. The Lords also refused Lin- coln's Inn Fields to be opened as playgrounds for the children and public of London ; they attempted to des- troy the charter establishing the University of Wales;they mutilated the scheme: of intermediate and technical schools under the Welsh Intermediate Education Act ; they rejected the Evicted Tenants Bill ; they attempted to destroy the Parish Councils Bill, and they excluded from the Scotch Local Government Bill the provision for I the compulsory acquisition by Parish Councils of land for I labourer's dwellings. That was only a small record of what the Lords lied prevented coming law ; it was only , a small part of the Liberal programme which the Lords had damned, and would again if they sent this coalition of Salisbury and Chamberlain to Westminster. They I would promise them anything but eive them nothing. (Applause.) They would give Ireland coercion for twenty years. What was it that the Irishman wanted ? He wanted nothing more or less than the right tc govern his own country, and to deal with those measures which appertained to Irishmen alone. (Cheers). The Bill of Mr. Gladstnne—(cheers)—did not impair the Imperial supremacy ; it did not in any way disintegrate the Em- pire, and did not march through rapine to that end. It was for them to decide the issue. (Loud cheers.) On Monday next, as they knew. they would have to go to the polling booth, and if they wanted beneficient legislation, legislation which should curtail the hours of labour ; which should en- deavour to make their homes in their old age better than they had been ; if they were going to have these benefits which he maintained were their due after a man had worked honestly and labnured hard during the best hours of life, and if they were going to look after their own interests, there was only one party for them to support, and that was the great Liberal party—(cheers) —which he had the honour to represent. (Cheers.) If they imagined that listening to him, and his speaking to them was going to win these things, they never made a greater mistake in the whole course of their lives. They would have to go out and work from now until next Monday. He did not want anybody to coerce, neither in any works or elsewhere. He wanted fair play and above board. If by legitimate action they could bring those on the ether side to see the error of their ways, he asked them to leave no stones unturned, and victory was theirs. (Loud and continued applause.)—Mr. Scott afterwards left the room to address another meet- ing:

I Mr. Toms GANDY, in moving a vote of confidence in Mr. Scott, said they all like their candidate, objected to the veto of the House of Lords. These men in the House of Lords to whom they should look for the greatest help in all questions affecting the well-being of the country, look dto their own interest—to the ques- tions affecting themselves, regardless of their effect on the working classes. So they had found themselves under the necessity of clipping their wings—(laughter) —and having men in Parliament who would do all they could to further their views and aspirations. He for one most strongly objeeted to what had been said by some shout Irishmen. RS refused to believe that Irishmen were not as good as any other men. In America. AustrSlia, and ether , parts of the world they found that Irishmen get the highest positions as legislators, as tradesmen, as flier- chants, and SS officers in the army and navy. (Ap- plause.) If Rome Rule would do anything to help them to develop those characteristics in their own country,bv all means let them have Home Rule and manage their own affairs. (Cheers.) Referring to workmen's dwellings he said his experience of buildina societies had taught him that instead of private individuals working together to secure here and there a house or a few houses, they might have building societies on a national scale. In- stead of private individuals having houses which were a disgrace to their civilisation, and in which men,women, and children herded together in unwholesome and un- healthy conditions, and they could by such a national system find dwellings for all the people, and they might be their own landlords. He considered the administra- tion of the poor laws a disgrace to a civilised country ; it was a great blot on the hospitality of a great nation towards its poorer members. (Applause.) Mr THOMAS MATHRR said it gave him great pleasure to second the resolution. He CAMP there that night to say a word on a special subject which seemed to be made a great point of at that election. He was as most of them knew, a Roman Catholic. (Applause.) Through- out the town there was placarded up a great "mani- festo" from the Roman Catholic hishops,of this country" and anyone reading that paragraph, would think that no Roman Catholic could vote for a Liberal candidate. But if they had read that manifesto they would know that there were four questions, and Mr. Pierpoint had simply taken a p.ragranh out of one of them. He (Mr. Mather) had had the pleasure r f introducing Mr. Scott to the Catholic clergy of Warrington. (Cheers.) He tackled Mr. Sc,stt first for his own satisfaction. There were some points, he must confess, that he did not thnrouchly understand. and he asked him to come to the clergy and have it nut with them. Like a man, Mr. Scott went, and argued nut the four vital points with them—(cheer.) with the result that he had the total sympathy of the Roman Catholic clergy in this fight. (Cheers.) He had quite satisfied them, and to all Catholics he would say, don't be afraid, have no hesita- tion, in %Wing for Mr. Scott. (Loud cheers) Mr KIERNAN said he Was very glad to listen to what Mr. Mather had said. He knew very well that the Catholics of Warrington were not going to vote for any Tories. (Rear, hear ) The Tories, when it suited their purpose would (mote from the Cardinal, or the Pope, or any of the high dignitaries of their church. It had been the custom of the old Tory party all theirlives. (Cheers.) They were only keeping up the ward " Tory" in its best and original sense. Everyone knew that the mean- ing of the word Tory was—a thief. (Heart hear and applause.) The Tories were" dab" hands at stealing things that would suit them at election times. But they mist not treat them as far as they could throw them. (Laughter ) They would do any mortal thing to get into office. They wotild take into their trust and council such men as Chamberlain (Cries of Judas.") They all knew what Birmineham was famed for. They polished brass to look like gold—(laughter)—and they polished their statesmen and nut a SDDrieUIS trade-mark upon them. (Renewed lataehter) lie would be ash ,ed to have anything to do with these Birmingham trt hers— everyone knew they were only veneered. (L ghter.) In Warrington the Liberals had got a young man, 27 years of age. He was not old enough to make many enemies, brit he was sore he would he SMe to make plenty of friends. It would be a disgrace to them if they did not win the election—(loud cheers)--nr at least reduce the majority. (Rear, hear, and loud cheers.) It they did not win they would fight another time until they had carried their cause forward to the greatest success ((Theers.) The speaker proceeded to deal with the 11011qe of Lords, and in conclusion urged all Trial,men to vote for Scott. (Cheers.) Mr. FARNSWORTH then addressed the meeting At length. He aisalr with the question of Home Rule for Ireland, and the action of the Heine of !Ards. He ap- pealed to N ,n-ronformists who were Unionists, to con- sider the n• her important measures besides Home Rule, which the Liberal n,rty had endeavoured to carry out. and were determined tn carry out. If they could not agree with them on Home Rule surely they must be with them on such a subject as disestabliahment, and he invoked them to support the candidature of Mr. Scott. (Cheers.)

The resolution was carried with acclamation.

Councillor HENRY ROBERTS said he did not intend to make a speech. It was not by speaking, but by working that the election would be won. (Cheers.) Mr. Scott came to Warrington on Saturday, a perfect stranger to everybody. But he thought he might say he was not a stranger now, and he was not sure whether he would not be more easily recognised by the majority of the people of Warrington, than even the ex-member for the Borough—Mr. Pierpoint, himself. (Applause.) He hoped they would give all the time they could to the important work of canvassing. 'nay had got a large number of honest Liberal electors is& Warrington. That meeting showed that the mak ad file was as honest and true as ever it was,and he wasted to get that clearly expressed at the poll. There wen 500 more electors this time than laat and that was where they had hope. (Applause.) They were going to redeem the Borough from the stain which had remained so long; and if they liked they could do it now by taking their fair share of the work. (Applause.) The meeting then terminated.

ANOTHER POISONING CASE,

What a sinister word : " Poisoned !" Yet : instances of poisoning occur daily, and in many of these cases life might have been saved if the proper antidote had been promptly used. There is no excuse for pleading ignor- ance, for most of the antidotes are simple and easily un- derstood. Capt. Jas. Paige, of Plymouth, was poisoned by one of the most common, but subtle poisons—uric acid—which att.lcks the blood and carries disease to I every part of the bystem. The only known and acknow- ledged antidote for "uric acid "poisoning :is Warner's Safe Cure, which stimulates the kidneys to remove these uric acid accumulations. An excess of uric acid in the system is easily recognised by occasional brick-dust de- posits, giddiness, muscular pains, feeling of depression, and lassitude, or a bitter taste in the mouth on rising, and at their appearance treatment should not be neglected " Yet; that is so," said Capt. Paige. " I never dreamed that my system was loaded with uric acid until tortured with muscular rheumatism and gravel. Pain was such I imagined myself spread upon the rack. My legs were swollen so that the skis cracked open ; my nervous sys- tem was shattered, my manhood seemed destroyed, and my reason almost dethroned. Then, after all other tried remedies and doctoring' failed, Warner's Safe Core cured me. This was eight years ago, and I have enjoyed good health since that time with the exception cf a severe attack of influenza."

• WARRINGTON.

-- BATHING FATALITY AT LITTLE SANKEY. A large number of boys, whose ages ranged from 9to 14 years of ages, were bathing in the River Mersey, at the place known as "Pool Mouth Brook' in Little Ssnkey when one of them, named George Thornl-,y, about eleven years of age (second son of Mr. James Thornley, waterman, of &trikey Bridges), cot beyond his depth and was drowned, and was afterwards carried down the river by the ebb tide. As soon as his companions saw him float- ing with the tide they were afraid and all ran away with the exception of one little fellow (cousin to the deceased), who watched him, about 400 yards, and as soon as the body disappeared he immediately went for the boy's clothes and then ran and gave information of the accident. A number of men at once proceeded in boats to the spot with grappling irons, and the body was found at about 9.30 p.m. the same day, in about 8 feet of water near the Cheveril.—The inquest was held on Monday after- noon at the Black Horse Inn, Sankey Bridges, by Mr. S. Brighouse.—James Tnornley, Waterman, Sloop-fold. Sankey, stated that the deceased was his son, whom he last saw alive at 230 on Saturday afternoon. At about four o'clock three boys brought to the house the clothes worn by the deceased, and said that he had been drowned whilst bathing. Witness and about a dozen other men began to drag for the body, and found it at about 9.30 some 300 yards from the point where the boys were said to have entered the water.—Ernest Seed, 13 years of age, and living at 129, Howard-st., Sankey Bridges. said he and two other boys went with the deceased to bathe in the river at a place known as Pool. mouth, Penketh. All the four of them entered the water, and deceased said he would try and walk across to the other side. When about half-way across he was carried away by the current and sank. They ',hen brought deceased's clothes home. Not one of the boys could swim.—A verdict of "Accidentally drowned" was returned. AmEarcks ROCK ER CHAIRS, sale price, 12s. 61. BA:HBO 3 TAI3LRS. Brighouse, coroner, conducted an enquiry at the Parade Room,lrlain-st.,on Monday,relative to the death of Peter Regan, 2 Margaret-st., on the sth inst. from the effects of a fall.—Mrs. Regan, deceased's wife, said he was 45 years of age and a Corporation labourer. When he came home on Tuesday last week he looked ill and said he had had a fall and cut his head. He afterwards became un- conscious and was on Wednesday removed to the Infirmary.—Peter Kenwright, 27 Winwick-road, a fellow workman of the deceased, said that when they finished their employment on Tuesday even- ing five of them went to witness's house when they had two quarts of beer between them. When de- ceased was about to leave he took a couple of steps towards the door and suddenly fell hack striking his head on the arm of a rocking chair. His head was cut, but whbn plaistered, deceased, who ap- peared right again, walked home. He was quite sober and there had been no quarrel or foul play at the house whatmver.—Thomas Thorley gave similar evidence.—Nurse Allen, from the infirmary, said that deceased retrained unconscious and died at one o'clock on Friday morning.—Several jurors testified to deceased's quietness of disposition and sobriety, and Inspector Norman observed that he bad been in the employ of the Corporation four or five yeara and was a respectable steady man.—A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned. On Saturdly the 3rd Battalion South Lancashire egi ment, which left Warrington for Altcar on the 10th June for the annual training, returned to town. The men left the camp at 8 a.m., and ar- rived in Warrington about half-past eleven. They looked very well their appearance having consider- ably improved during their stay in camp. The regiment on reaching Orford Barracks was " paid off" and dismissed. WARRINGTON WOMAN IN TROUBLE AT WIDNES. At the Widnes Police Court, on Friday, before Mr. Sadler, Margaret Lynch. who said that she came from Warrington, was charged with creating a breach of the peace on the previous evening.— E Award Ranet said that on the night in question about eight o'clock pritoner came to his house,fand demanded lodgings. lAUTION —Beware of buying new London jerrybuilt fur- niture, no matter how low the price is— it is dear at any price. Worsley's have second-hand reliable furniture by good makers, which will last for ages. There is no disputing the fact that high-class second-band furniture is better than new. For instance, second-hand side- boards are easily modernised by having the backs raised, the plates bevelled, and the panels carved. Worsiey s have, there- fore, erected a Cabinet Works in City-road, with an immense stock of seasoned timber for the production of reliable furni- ture. Every item in the Bedding Department is new, gu iron- teed pure, under minute inspection.

COMPLETH FURNISHING ORDRES.—Worsley's cater specially for Complete Furnishing Orders, supply estimates free, ani believe in the equity of supplying com- plete orders at a less ratioof prat than single articles.

As the election proceeds there is a brighter pros- pect of a heavy labour poll in favour of Liberal candidates. Apart from the members of the Inde- pendent Labour Party who declare that they will vote for neither Liberal nor Tory, the trades' unions and other labour combinations are rallying to the side of the Liberals in an enthusiastic fashion. They find that they have nothing to expect from Lord Salisbury and the Duke of Devonshire, the guiding spirits of the new government, while they are encouraged by the record of the late Liberal Government. To the colliers Mr. Thomas Ashton the General Secretary of the Miners' Federation, writes" Linust call your attention to the resolution passed at the annual conference of the Federation lin January last, that we support those candidates. only who will vote for the will in its entirety, through all its stages in the House of Commons. The Eight Hours Bill must extend its operations as far as the Mines' Regulation Act. No local option No district to be exempt from the Bill. No handi- capping. All collieries to work the same time. Eight hours from bank to bank. Let us enter into this contest with an earnest determination to win and we shall succeed."

There are now the most unmistakeable signs that as one writer puts it, a " dry rot" has set in amongst the Liberal Unionists. Those Liberal Unionists who were not downright Tories to com- mence with, now feel that they have been betrayed and deluded by the leaders who have clambered into office in a Tory Government over their backs. They now find that they are expected to back up Toryism pure and simple, not only in regard to Ire- land but " all round," and this is what those of them who have any vestiges of Liberalism in their composition cannot stomach. Hence the rapid series of conversions—or is it reversions P—to Liberalism of prominent Unionists in different parts of the country.

We should think that there are few Irishmen who can be in any doubt as to wl4ich party is most entitled to their votes, but those who are should take note of the manifesto issued to the Irish Nationalists of Great Britain by the Irish National League. In this spirited document Irishmen are told that" What a Tory Government would do for the redress of our grievances and other inequalities, both political and religious, they had seen at the very moment of their attainment to power. The Bill which would have enfranchised many thousands of Catholic people in Ireland had been rejected by the Lords, and no Popery and Orange ascendency in Ireland had had yet another trivaiph." The Nationalists are therefore asked "to return to power the Liberal party, the party of Home Rule, which had never yet taken up any great cause without ultimately carrying it to vic- tory."

There can be no disputing the fact that Mr. Moon is making a splendid fight for the Newton division. The constituency has never, we should think since it was formed, been canvassed and worked so thoroughly on behalf of the Liberal candidate as it is being this time. What is more, the canvassers' reports are, even from the districts considered to be the most hopelessly Tory, dis- tinctly encouraging. TOere has undoubtedly been apolitical awakening amongst the working people of the constituency, a fact which is no doubt argely due to the operations of the Parish Coun- cils Bill. The voters have begun to realise the utter barrenness of Mr. Legh's Toryism—and Mr Legh's Toryism is a barren sort—and they will no doubt act accordingly.

It appears from what Mr. Moon said at Padgate on Wednesday that the word " reform " has dropped by some accident or other into Mr. Legh's address. Mr. Legh has expressed himself in favour of "tem- perance reform " but it is said that when he was asked at a meeting what he meant by "temperance reform" he cogitated for a time and then declared that he must defer his answer till another occasion. Well, now, that is exactly what might have been expected from Mr. Legh under the circumstances' When that other occasion comes the answer will be in the highest degree interesting. One feels posi- tively eager to hear what Mr. Legh considers to be temperance reform.

THE LIBERAL RECORD. TWENTY THINGS DONE.

Since the Liberals came into power in 1892 they have 1. Carried through the House of Commons a just and generous measure of Home Rule for Ireland which would be jaw to-day but for a few hundred Unionist peers. _ . . . .

2. Passed-two Parish Council Acts, giving to the people of our villages in England and Scotland the right to manage their own affairs, and placing on a popular basis all over England the election of Poor-law Guar- dians.

3. Carried through the House of Commons the Em- ployers' Liability Bill, but the House of Lords insisted on allowing "contracting out," so that the Bill, which was thus made worthless, had to be withdrawn. 4. Passed an Act to regulate the Hours of Railway Servants, which has bettered the condition of thousands' of railway men. 5. Passed an Act to provide for the Educationof Blind and Deaf Children. 6. Passed an Act to increase the sums allowed as de- posit in the Post Office Savings Bank. 7. Pas ed the Budget of 1894, in which it was laid down for the first time that extra taxation ought to be borne by those who can best afford to pay. 8. Passed the Equalisation of Rates (London) Act, by which the poorer London districts are relieved of rating at the expense of the richer, which have hitherto escaped their fair share of payment. 9. Passed an Act compelling employers to notify accidents.

10. Passed an Act giving increased protectioa to shareholders in Building Societies against fraud. 11. Passed an Act to make additionally safe the lives and limbs of those working in quarries. 12. Insisted that every child whose parents asked for it shall have Free Education, and that school build- ings shall be healthy, of sufficient size, properly drained, _ . _

13. Appointed a number of practical workine,-men to be Assistant Factory Inspectors, and for the first time appointed Women Inspectors for the occupations in which women are engaeed. 14. Made inquiry into dangerous and unhealthy trades, so that rules may be formed to protect the workers. .

15. Increased the wages of workmen employed by the Government by £30,000 a year ; reduced the hours of labour to eight hours a day for every man and boy em- ployed in the factories and workshops of the War Office, Post Office, and in the dockyards ; and given Trades Union wages and Trade Union hours to the workmen at South Kensington and Bethnal-green. 16. Created a tabour Department of the Board of Trade with a monthly " Labour Gazette," giving full in- formation on the state of employment in the United Kingdom ; and assisted with the help of Lord Rosebery and the Labour Department, in settling the great coal dispute of 1893, and the great 13oot dispute in 1895 17. App -anted a number of Working men Magistrates in the Boroughs,and commenced to deal with the County Benches. 18. Increased the facilities, for investing and with- drawing money from the 13:-ist Office Savings Bank, and for transmitting seamen's Wages, extending the system to foreign parts. , 19. Abolished the rule forbidding Government employiSs to join trades unions. 20. Appointed Royal Commissiens to inquire into Agricultural Depression and the coadition of the Aged Poor.

THE LIRERAL PROGRAMME. TWENTY THINGS TO BS DONE. 1. To secure the passing into law of the some Rule Bills 2. To secure the passing into law of the Employers' Liability Rill. [Nese two Bills were bet through the action of the Lords.] 3. To Disestablish and Disendow the Church •ot England in Wales, and also the EStablished Church in Scotland. 4. To amend the Land Laws of Ireland so as to give additional security to the tenant for his own improve. ments, and to make proviirion for the reinstatement of Evicted Tenants. 5. To amend the Law of Registration, so that every man who is entitled shall have a vote, to secure " One Man One Vote," audd.fotold all Elections on one and the same day.

power with the richest man in the district in the man- ) agemeat of their local affairs had raised them in their • own self-esteem and made them proud to be citizens. I (Cheers.) They were promised by the Unionists a Gov- ernment of social reform, but what had they had at the bands of the late Government but social reform ? (Cheers.) Was not the Local Government Act social re- form P Was not the regulation of the hours of railway. servants social reform? Was not the Factories Act social reform? Was not security for market gardeners social reform? The late Government promised an Employers' Liability Bill ; and a Local Control of the Liquor Traffic Bill. Were not those social reforms? Could they imagine a more hopeful social reform than the administration, regulation, and control of the trade by the veto of the people, instead of irresponsible magistrates P They bad also a bill for the salvation of the evicted tenants of Ireland—was not that again social reform P Then they had Mr. George Lambert's Land Tenure Bill to give security to English farmers which passed the House of Commons but got no furthee. They promised social reform in the future. (Cheers). But what did Sir Michael Hicks-Beach, when speaking the other day at Bristol, say about the Unionist policy ? —"What was to be the policy of the Unionist party ? He must not go too far as to that "—(laughter)—" but of this he could assure them, that their policy would be exactly opposite to that of their predecessors ! " (Laughter.) They knew what the policy of their pre- decessors had been. Sir William Harcourt had to pro- vide for enormous expenditure by the Government, and his bearers knew how that expenditure was provided for. For the first time in the history of this country it was provided for by a process which taxed the rich and relieved the poor. (Applause.) But Sir Michael Hicks- Beach told them that the Unionist policy was to be ' exactly opposite. They were going to tax the poor instead of the rich. That was a serious matter. They might depend upon it that those words of Sir Michael Hicks-Beach, though they might sound somewhat jocular, had a very serious meaning behind ' them. The Unionist policy would, in most particulars, I be the exact opposite to that of the late Government. It I always was the case. The leopard could not change his 4 spots or the Ethiopian his skin. The farmers were to I have no security, employers were to be permitted to screw their men into contracting out of compensation for accidents, the Welsh farmers were to continue to pay ' tithes to a church they never went to, and the owners ' of personal property are to be made to pay more in order 4 that the great landowners, the great brewers, and the great alkali makers too—(laughter)—might pay less. Let them be careful that they did not return a Tory I majority, because if they did they might depend upon it I that tho great landowners, big brewers,and the wealthy I men generally, would pay them back for what Sir Wm. 1 Harcourt did last year. A coinblaint had been made 4 that the late Government had been pampering the I working classes. He contended that it had been a I Government of fair play :all round. Complaints had ; also been made that the working classes had been too grasping in their demands, and too exclusively selfish ia 1 the consideration of their own interests, but when they could match ameng the working-classes the selfishness 1 of the House of Lards—(cheers)--he would believe that accusation. The Government that he was proud to serve was defeated by a back-door majority. He did not , watch the operation himself but he was told that a con- ' 1 siderable number got into the House of Commons by an , ' unusual way where there were no Government whips to , watch them. He considered that was a bad beginning for a new Government, and if the business of the coun- try was to be carried on in that petty, malignant spirit, they would be far from being proud of it. (Applause.) ; He had been proud to represent the Northwich consti- tuency. There was ns constituency that he knew of that he would rather represent. (Hear, hear.) He was proud of its Liberalism and of the enterprise and public spirit shown in the management of local affairs in the various towns in the division. He referred to the chief , institutions under public management in the several 1 towns, and in speaking of Runcorn,after mentioning the Free Library, he said it had set an example in sanitation 1 and had greatly diminished the InJrtality in con- sequence of the great careand pains that had been taken. I Runcorn had also recently purchased the water works. He could tell them that no man was prouder of his con- stituency than he who had been their member for the last ten years. (Applause.) Referring to his address to the electors he said he had put his whole heart into it, and he trusted that when it reached their homes it would reach their hearts too. (Loud applause.) Sir John Brunner afterwards proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Speakman and Mr. Bratt (the chairman of the Liberal Council, and the Chairman of the • Executive) for their services, and in doing so, paid a trihute to their labours in the Liberal cause in the divi- sion.—Mr. A. Fletcher seconded the resolution, which on being put to the meeting was carried with acclamation. —Mr. Speakman responded and the meeting then ' terminated.

PUBLIC MEETING AT NORTHWICII. On Tuesday evening a crowded and enthusiastic meeting was held at Northwichin support of Sir John Brunner's candidature. A vote of confidence in Sir John was passed on the motion of Dr. Bowman, seconded by Mr. Parker Lowe.—Sir John Brunner, referring to his active support of the Compensation Act and the re- formation of the River Weaver Trust, said he appeared before them in a spirit of cheerful satisfaction, and therefore he began the contest in a spirit of high good humour. He declared himself as gmd a Home Ruler as ever, and his views had become broader and more com- prehensive. Instead of claiming Home Rule for Ireland, lie would claim Horne Rule for Scotland, Wales, and, not the least, for England. (Cheers.) He recognised that there were matters upon which their views were not identical. hut these were dealt with in the bill before the House of Commons. He was satisfied that that system of Home Rule was the sound and true one which pro- vided that in matters cbncerning individual action alone each nation should be supreme within its own sphere. (Cheers ) Speakinz in favour of local control of the liquor traffic, he said he would not pledge himself against all schemes of compensation to the publican. He was a temperance reformer, and if it was necessary in order to obtain temperance reform to compensate publicans ot a moderate ertent he would vote for compensation, in spite of the fact that most temperance reformers woud _ .

veto of the House a Lords, not as a revolutionist but as a consistant reformer. Upon this greatly to be desired reform, hang all others—Home Rule for all the three kingdoms and fer Wales—reform of the land laws, involving security for the farmer and for every other trading tenant—the transfer of the power to regu- late and control the traffic in intoxicating liqnors from the hands of irresponsible magistrates into those of the people themselves—protection of fife and health for workpeople—equality of voting power in the election of national representatives—disestablishmerit of the Welsh Church, and all measures for promoting the social amelioration of the people." WIDNES DIVISION ELECTION.

Mr. Deacon, the Liberal candidate, concluded his tour of the wards on Wednesday, addressing meetings in Victoria, Waterloo, and West Bank Wards. The attend- ance at all three meetings was large, and the proceed- ings most enthusiastic and unanimous. The Conservative candidate, Mr. J. S. Gilliat, opened his campaign on Thursday night, speaking at the Mission Hall, West Bank, the small room being well filled.—On Thursday Mr. Rutherford. of the firm of Messrs. Miller, Peel, Hughes, Rutherford, and Co., solicitors, Cook-st., Liver- pool, who will act for the High Sheriff during the elec- tion, was in Widnes, and made arrangements for the polling on Friday week. Upon this occasion there will be a polling station in each of the six wards of the borough, instead of three stations, which were allotted to Widnes at the last election. The nominations take place on Monday next between the hnurs of eleven and 011 P. MR. DEACON'S CAMPAIGN. The first of the Liberal meetings was held at Lacey- street Schools, Dr. O'Keeffe presiding over a good at- tendance. . .

Mr. Deacon, who was cordially received, said he was quite certain that the division would be won for Liberalism. If Widnes wished to return him they could do it, but if they did not do so it would be because the honour of representing Widnes as a Liberal was to be for some one else, and some other man would have a chance in the future. He appealed to those present to be united. There were in the Liberal party (and there always would be in the party of progress) many diverse views, but if his (Mr. Deacon's) opinions were on the whole satisfactory to them, be trusted they would sink small differences for the sake of the great object they all had in view, and vote for him, although they might not agree upon every point. He promised if elected to give careful at- tention to local affairs, and said he might fairly claim to know more about Widnes than his opponent, Mr. Oi liat. He expressed himself in favour of pensiqns for the aged por, but said he would not pledge himself to any parti- cular measure, as at present there was no sceeme before the public which could net be riddled by criticism. He expressed himself in favour of the Local Option Bill, hut regretted that a scheme of temperance reform should have been made a party question. It ought to be above party politics. (Applause.) Colonel Biggs and Mr. Max Muspratt afterwards addressed the meeting.

. - The second meeting was held in S. Patrick's School, and here also there WAS a crowded attendance, over which Mr. A. Borthwic.k presided.—The Candidate, who had another flattering reception, said if they all did their best they would win. So far as it depended on him, he meant to do everything possible, and he asked them to fight in such numbers as to give them the victory. (Applause.) He then dealt with Irish questions, and criticised the action of the House of Lords in rejecting the Irish Municipal Franchise Bill. The House of Lords had always been unfair and unjust to Ireland. The last of the series was held at the Oakland street Schools. Mr. I. Hartland presided over a good attend- ance. Mr. Deacon dealt with the fallacy that trade was had when the Liberals were in power and good daring the Tory reign,dealt with social legislation,and criticised his opponent's election address, which stated that the Unionist party were especially qualified to deal with social questions from their past record. Votes of confidence were carried at each of the meet- ngs.

THE ELECTIONS.

NORTHWICH DIVISION ELECTION.

OPENING OF VIE CAMPAIGN.

The Conservative Association in the Northwich divi- sion have at last been successful in selecting a candidate to oppose Sir John T. Brurner in the forthcoming politi- cal battle. Being unable to secure Mr. Joseph Verdin, who declined to stand on account of ill-health, Mr. Thomas Ward, a director of the Salt Union of North- wich, was asked to step into the breach. He acceded to the request, and a contest will now take place. Sir John T. Brunner was Unanimously recommended to the Libe- ral Council as the Liberal candidate at the ensuing elec- tion.

Since tbe wo parties drew into line on Saturday last the preparations for the battle which is to be decided on Wednesday, the 24th lost, has gone on apace, and every effort will be made to get the 11,788 electors to the poll on that date. Mr. William flandley,of Runcorn, has been appointed agent for Sir John Brunner,and Mr. Handley's arrangements on the occasion of the last general election drew forth high encomiums from Sir Jahn Causton. Mr. J. S. Hughes, of North wich,is the agent for Mr. Thomas Ward, J.P., the Unionist candidate. Several meetings have been held in different parts of the constituency dur- ing the week by both sides,the meeting of Sir John Brun- ner at North wich on Tuesday evening being a decided success. As will be seen from advertisements in another column two political meetings are to be held in Runcorn next week; one in support of the candidature of Sir John Brunner, to be held on Monday evening in the Public Hall, and the other on Wednesday evening in the same hall in support of the Unionist candidate, Mr. Thomas Ward, J.P. SPEECH BY SIR JOHN T. BRUNNER.

A meeting of the Liberal party was held at the Glad- stone Club,Northwich,ot Saturday afternoon. The chair was occupied by Mr. Philip Speakman, J.P., C.C., and there were present representatives from all parts of the constituency. From Runcorn there were present in- cluding the Chairman :—Rev. J. Stafford, Messrs. T. Handley, J.P., R. H. Posnett, A. R. Norman, William Handley, Carroll, J. R Frith, G. H. Kirkham, J. Wilson, J. McCulloch, Joseph Stubbs, E. Gatley, R. Burrows, W. E Green, James Lea, J. Billington, W. Millington, J. M. Frith, A. Riley, John Clarke, T. Furnival, S. Webster, Johnson, and others. In opening the proceedings the Chairman thanked the members for the honour they conferred upon him at the annual meeting by appointing him their chairman. He thought the faces he saw around him augured success in the forthcoming contest. He (the chairman) was at the helm for the time being of their vast organisation and he felt mire that the workers he saw before bins would put their hands to the plough and would n turn back until they had gained that, end which they had set themselves out to accomplish. (Applause.) The Executive Committee had adopted once more Sir J. T. Brunner to be the candidate to represent the Liberal interest in the Northwich division. (Applause.) If they locked hack upon what he had done during the last nine years he thought they could conscientiously support him. There was a high moral tone in all Mr. Brunner had done for them. For the List 40 years he had been a worker in the Liberal cause and he was proud to sup- port Sir John Brunner. He had stuck to them right 'along, and he (the chairman) trusted that it was their wish that Sir John Brunner should represent them laeain. The way to fight the contest was to fight it in a gentlemanly way. (Hear, hear.) He thought, who- ever the candidate might be on the opposite side, there was not the slightest nerd for them to quarrel over their opponent and he trusted their opponeets would keep off personalities. Brunner, M.P., for his great and unwearied service as member for Parliament for the Northwich division ; that he be respectfully requested to again consent to be its candidate at the general elec- tion ; and further that he be assured of the determina- tion of the Liberal parts, to secure by all legitimate means his triumphant return."—The resolution was se- conded by Mr. W. WARRINGTON ELECTION.

Contined from page 8.

OPEN AIR MEETING IN BEWSEY-ROAD.

MR. PIERPOINT'S ACTION ON THE CONCILIATION AND EMPLOYERS LIABILITY BILLS. A large open air meeting was held on the vacant piece of ground opposite St. Paul's Church, Bewsey-road, on Wednesday noon, in support of the candidature of Mr. Scott. The cheir was occupied by Mr. T. B. Malkin (president of the Warrington Trades Council). The CHAIRMAN said that they had once more the oppor- tunity of selecting a member to represent Warrington in the House of Commons. It was for the electors of War- rington to say whether they were satisfied with the action of the member for Warrington during his term of office. He might say that the late Government was the most democratic Government that this country ever had —(hear, hear)—and had passed many useful reforms which, however, had been rejected by the House of Lords. The curious cabinet which had just been formed talked about social reform, but he was afraid that they would get very little social reform from them. (Applause.) Among many Bills which had been brought in by the Government, had been the Local Government Bill, which was one of the finest bills ever introduced into Parliament for workingmen. who thus got local con- trol in the villages which made them equal to their so- called superiors. Then the Factory Act was a measure of social reforpn,and that had been passed through Parlia- ment. The Conciliation Bill was a most useful Bill and that had been introduced into the House of Commons by the Government, and he was sorry that their late mem- ber—Mr. R. Pierpoint—had blocked the progress of the Bill and thus it had not been passed. What did concilia- tion mean ? It meant that workmen and employers should meet in conference, and discuss any reasonable method for settling any trade dispute. What had con- ciliation done in the late miners' strike? It ha l brought about peace and satisfaction to both parties. This had also been the Case in the Leicester strike. The object of the Conciliation Bill was to extend this privilege to every trade and business. (Applause.) 'Thu there was the Employers' Liibility Bill. This bill was wrecked owing to the action of the House of Lords. The Conservatives were asking the working-men for their votes, but he contended that they were not the friends of the working men. They did not want the working-men except at election times, and then they only wanted their votes. If the working-men wanted payment of meinhere, so that they could be represented in Parliament by their own class, they should vote for the Liberal party. Why should not they have payment of members. (Applause.) They had Men in Parliament getting £5,000 a year, and yet these men objected to others receiving £250 a year. (Applause.) Mr, FARNSWORTH, of the National Reform Union, then addressed the meeting. He said that it was a very satis- factory thing that the chairman of that meeting should be a working-man, as they could through him, appeal to the other working-men. The nresent contest was the greatest they had had since 1832 as there were some of , the gravest issues to be decided. The late Radical Governmeet had been one of the most beneficent of medern times, and Lord Rosebery (applause) upon whose shoulders the mantle of Mr. Gladstone (applause) , had fallen, was still carrying out the great remedial reforms which that great statesman had so long been carrying forward. They had the evidence of Mr. Joseph Chamberlain that th 3 administration of the last Government during the period of office had not been equalled. The taunt was often flung at the late Government that they had wasted their time in the House of Come-none and done nothing. They had however passed many great measures, and if they had not beceme law, it was not their fault, but that of the irresponsible Chamber—the House of Lords. Mr. Pierpoint during the time he had been mem- ber for Warrington had voted against every democratic measure, and everything which was for the welfare of the working classes. (Hear, hear.) He had supported the contracting out clause, which had rendered the Em- ployers' Liability Bill useless. But for this, the Bill would have become law,and have been one of the great- est boons to the working classes that had ever been passed. The late Government had beers the best for labour as it supported eight hours—(applause)—and had extended the principle to the dock yards and arsenals. The question at present before the country was whether they were going to be controlled by the House of Lords. That MRS!, had destroyed Bill after Bill which had been sent up from the House of Commons. With regard to the closure, of which so much had been made by the Tories, he would tell them that that was a legacy left from the Tories, and the Liberals had not used it nearly so often as their opponents. The battle was fought upon this question, and he had not the slightest doubt as to the issue,and it remained for Warrington to do its duty by sending Mr. Scott, a man of whom Manchester was proud, and who had been so successful in the commer- cial world—to Parliament. (Loud applause.) Mr. Scaler, who had a very encouraging reception ,said that it struck him that if he spoke long and they did not get to their work,they would find the closure somewhere else. (Laughter.) He would tell them why he was pre- sent at that meeting. It was because their political op- ponents said that he was not one of them. Well,he was with them (his hearerseand what was more, he intended to be much more -with them in the future. (Applause.) Whether next Monday showed that he was their member or not, that would not make him back out of Warring- ton. (Renewed applause.) Warrington should be Liberal whether it was next Monday, or in the future, and they would find one thing,that if next Monday, the issue was against them, they would very soon have to say what it was to be again. (leppleuse.) They would have to tackle first-, the House of Lords. (Hear,hear.) Secondly, they wanted one man one vote. Ile failed to see, and he believed his hearers failed to see why, wealth and aristocracy should be represented'by perhaps- ten votes to their one. (Hear, hear.) They never would get full liberty unless that were altered: As to the Registration Law Amendment , At, he thought that the qualification of the period should be roducedlonot more than three months—(ap.

===== 0002601_18950713_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
plause), and once a voter, always a voter. (Applause.) He would have an opportunity of addressing them on other occasions,but he asked for their assistance in in the people's battle, and he believed it was the cause of freedom and of right,and they would only have them- selves to blame if they did not win. (Applause.) Mr. MALKIN proposed a vote of confidence is Mr. Scott and said that during the past year there had been more coal produced than ever before, and he asserted that it was a mistake to suppose that a Tory Government brought good trade. Re hoped that the electors of War- rington would put Mr. Scott at the head of the poll on Monday. (Applause.) The resolution was carried unanimously, amid much cheering, and the bell ringing for the resumption of work after dinner hour, the crowd quickly dispersed.

LIBERAL MEETING IN LATCHFORD WARD.

On Wednesday evening a meeting in support of the candidature of Mr. Scott, the Liberal candidate, was held in the Primitive Methodist Chapel, Latchford,under the chairmanship of Councillor William Pierpoint, J.P., and notwithstanding the fact that Latchford ward is considered a Conservative stronghold there was an excel- lent attendance of electors who displayed nlenty of enthusiasm. On the platform, supporting the Chairman there were Mr. Farnsworth, of the National Reform Union, Mr. James Horton, and later on Mr. Percival R. Scott,on whose appearance in company with Councillor H. Roberts there was raised an enthusiastic burst of applause. The CHAIRMAN said that he had to explain that they were holding two meetings that night, one at Brick-st. schoolroom at which Mr. Scott was then speaking. (APplause.) While Mr. Scott was there they had a good friend of his, Mr. Farnsworth of Manchester, who had come to Warrington out of consideration for Mr. Scott's delicate health, following his illness, to speak at public meetings. It was only at the urgent desire of the Liberal party that Mr. Scott ventured to take upon himself the arduous duties of fighting the constituency for Parlia- mentary honours. He (thechairman) did not know why the Liberal workingmen of Warrington and of Latchford should take upon themselves to support Mr. Robert Pier. point. He did not know that there was anything in Mr. Pierpoint's policy—or programme, for it, was hardly A policy—that appealed very strongly to workingmen, and he hoped that the working-men of Latchford would take his speeches and address and compare them with the address and speeches that have been and would be made by the Liberal candidate. Mr. Scott. (Applause.) The speeker wanted them to think out the matter for them- selves as it was their battle that Mr. Scott and the Liberal party were fighting. (Applause.) It had been said that it was a great presumption on their part in offering to contest the town, but he would like to point nut that since the last election there had been an addi- tional number of over 500 voters added to the register, in ether word's there were now 500 more voters than then. He thought it would have been an act of moral coward- ice on their part if they had not offered the 500 extra voters an opportunity of testifying their opinions. (Hear, hear.) It might be possible that out of the 500 extras 300 might be in favour of their programme. (Applause.) If that was so and they could turn over a few others from their Tory friends' list of last election then they would he juetified by their success in bringing about a contest in Warrington. (Applause.) For his part he was quite willing to take his share of the blame, if there was any blame attributable to them am a party. He Was not ashamed of the action he had taken in doing what he could to find Mr. Robert Pierpnint an opponent worthy of his steel. (Applause). There was one point he would like to mention, raised to his mind by the presence of Mr. S. B. Stringer. (Applause.) That was that with reference to the Local Vets Bill They knew that Mr. Stringer was a man who had worked very hard during the winter in getting up evening concerts— (applause)—in order to provide a counter attrac- tion to public-houses in the town. He was very glad to say that Mr. Stringer'a efforts were well rewarded because he had had crc wded houses almost every Saturday night. On one occasion he (the Chair- man) had the pleasure of presiding, and he was more than gratified at the large number of young men and women present; and the excellent entertainment placed at their disposal for enjoyment. The speaker added that he was not a teetotaller himself, and he expected that a great number of the men in the room were aware of the fact, and some of them might think that because he was not he had -hardly a right to be on that platform when one of the main items on their address was the Local Veto Bill, which he approved of. and which gave the workingmen the power to decide whether they would have public-houses or not. They could depend neon it that they would have a large sprinkling of tee- totallers in their locality, and a larger number who were not total abstainers. It would be their own fault,there- fore. if publie-houss were &Ms away with against their wish, but certainly they could do with a great deal less in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) Another point with re- ference to publie-hrmsee : he was rather in favour of the eld.faehioneri nublic-houses which they used to have in Warrington 30 years ago ; when the man who owned the public-house held the license himself. Since the ad- vent of the two large brewers, Messrs. Walker and Greenall, the old system, with the exception of two howee, had been swept away, and the remaining li- eeneee in Warrington had been abeerhed by these firma. Ile believed he was right in saving that there was not 50 per cent of the public-house keepers in Warrington doing more than just Paving their way and getting a mere pittance. Local Veto offered the power to people in localities to determine for themselves what they would have in the shape of drink-honsee. (Applause.) Mr. FARNSWORTH' said he had great pleasure in partici- nating in Mr. Scott's candidature for this conetituency. They in Manchester believed that Mr. Scott was a man peculiarly fitted for a constituency like Warringtoe. He was a man of large sympathies, a man of good social standing, a man who had given no lack of study and studious consideration to thnem great pressing questions of the day. Not only did they belieye that Mr. Scott was thus fitted to represent a working class constituency of this nature but they were looking to this constituency that it should on Mon lay next reverse the order of Hro- Haedirles and assert its Liberalism. (Applause.) Their I Tory friends had had their innings in this constituency. (Hear, hear.) This contest he belieeed would be the 'hottest and sharpest on record. It would be futile for him to meddle with what was now ancient political his- tory and detail the course of events which had brought , about the contest. Suffice it for him to say that from his knowledge of the Radical wing in Warrington there was a spirited policy, a policy growing hourly, an en. trmsiagm such as had not been felt,or meen,or understood for years, an enthusiasm which was practically a conse- cration of the working elms energies to redeem this con- stituency from the foul epith•ta that could be hurled at

it, The spesker then went on in & very vigorous speech to expound the Objecta of the Libertl progtammn, and to show the stumbling blocks which the House of Lords alwav put in the wav of pronressive legislation. He also dealt largely with flume Rule and the peacefulness of Ireland due to Liberal Government. The CHAIRMAN' at the conclu'don of Mr. Farnsworth's speech. said the justification for brinpinp about, this con- test had been well shown. STRINGRR seconded the motion and held it to he more honourable to stand defeated with clean hands than successfull with dirty hands. He honed can gassing would soon be done away with, and he called upon all to go like men to the poll and vote for the party which had dealt out to them the blessings they now enjoyed. He had great pleasure in seconding the resolution. A show of hande was taken, and the feeling of the meeting was unanimous there being not one dissentient hand raised. The resolution was therefore declared carried amidst loud and continued appfause Mr. Sgorr thanked the meeting for the kind way in which it had received the resolution, and remarked that he would never abuse their confidence if he had it on Monday next. (A0,11111.e.) The CHORMAN said that though her should be glad to see canvassing done away with himself they could not do without it now, and he urged all Liberals to do their utmost to secure votes for Mr. Scott. The meeting then terminated.

The railway companies of Great Britain pay a daily averare of onts thousand seven hundred pounds in com- pensation, about sixty per cent. being for injuries to passengers, and the remainder for lost or damaged freight. •

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

" CRYING "AND USING OBSCENE LANGUAGE IN THE MARKET. On Monday morning, at the Warrington Borough Police Court, before the Mayor and other magistrates, Thomas Clare, 5, Scotland-Nati, and James Daniels, 37, Howard-street, Manchester, were each charged with Crying fish for sale in the market contrary to the bye- laws of the borough. Daniels was further charged with using obscene language there on the 27th June.—P.C. Corbett stated that from orders he had received from the Inspectors to suppress shouting he listened for some time to the defendants shouting and P.C. Gibbs coming up he remained to corroborate. They cautioned the de- fendants but Mr. Lever, their employer told them to keep on shouting as the police were "only a lot of bullies put on by other fish dealers." A crowd gathered round and when witness attempted to disperse them Lever told them to stay as ' he and his men could best the police." Witness afterwards secured defendants' names and addresses and reported the case.—P.C. Gibbs corro- borated.—Mr. Lever who Was present gave evidence for the defence and denied the imputations of the police but at the same time made another charge against them which brought him a reprimand from the Bench.—Both defendants were fined 53. including costs for " crying " and Daniels for the obscene language was fined a further ss. and costs.

A NEGLECTFUL MOTHER DESERVEDLY PUNISHED.—EIIen Moran, a widow, of 1 Salkeld-sqnare, was summoned to answer a charge preferred against her by Inspector McAteer, of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, for having on the Ist July neg- lected her children in a manner likely to cause unneces- sary suffering or injury to their health. She was further charged with indecent behaviour on the 6th Inspector McAteer gave an account of his visit to the defendant's house in company with Ser- geant Lee. He found four of the children in the house in a most deplorable state of neglect ; without sufficient' clothing and food and in a filthy condition. Another child was kept by her grandmother in Warrington. In answer to his question as to how she supported herself and children she admitted that it was by prostitution. He asked her to let the children go to the Workhouse but this she declined to do. The children had been grossly and systematically neglected. They had been turned out to beg late at night. It was one of the wurst eases he had had since he had been in the Warrington district. The defendant was a drunken woman and she was bring- ing up the family in immoral surroundings. He asked the magistrates to make an example of her.—Sergeant Lee corroborated the Inspector's statemeet, and said the children were continually neglected.—P.C.'s Richardson and Ridgeway also gave evidence for the prosecution. The second portion of the charge was proved by P.C. Richardson The Chairman said the magistrates found the defendant guilty of the two charges, and said such a dreadful condition of things was something appalling in a Christian community. They had decided to send her to prison for three months with hard labour for the first offence and fourteen days for the second. The children would be sent to the workhouse. _ . .

MESSRS. PETER WALKER. AND SON'S ANNUAL TRIP.

The annual trip promoted hv thiq firm was held on Saturday, the destination being Douglas, Isle of Mao. which selection in the eiel proved an exceeding happy one for all concerned. Tirl weather was beautiful and the water most reassuring!y smooth. _

A start was made from 'Cie Central Station at 5 20 a.m. when about 750 expectant trippers lett for Liverpool. By the kind permission of Lieutenant-Colonel Ridgway and officers ot the Ist V. B. (Prince of Wales Volunteers) South Lancashire Regiment, the band, under the able leadership of Brigade Bandmaster Armstrong, accom- panird the excursion. Oa arrival at Liverpool, and on the way to the steamer, they struck up the well-known tune "hail, smiling morn." Rugle-Major Plinston was likewise among the band, accupying his old position in capital style. 'flie steamer King Orry had been specially chartered for the occasion, and was moored to the Landing Stage, whither the excursiodists made the best of their way, marching in the rear of the bandsmen. Music was also provided on hoard the vessel which was soon out: to sea with all on hoard.

Your hours sailing brought the party to their destina- tion and afterwards a hot luncheon was served in the , large saloon below to the officials of Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons and several guews. Amongst these who sat down to this rnt at were—the Chairman, Councillor Baird; Alderman Frain:tomb and Wright, Mr. A. F. G. Potter, Mr. William James, Liverpool ; Mr. John J. Dun, King- street brewer ; Mr. 1. C. Brookes,Mr. Cox, R. Ross,fore- Mae of departments ; Professor Grant, managing direc- tor of Falcon Cliff Gtrdens ; Mr. Woods, London and North-Western Railwaa's Company's agent at Warring- ton; Mr. Thompson, Midland Railway Coonpany's agent; Mr. Rigby, Cheshire Lines agent; Mr. Abdy, Great Wes- tern Railway agent; many of the exercise officers attached to the Brewery Company, Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, &c. Many letters of apology had been received, some of which were from Mr. Alexander Bruce, ,chairman director of the Diuglas Electric Tr smear Company ; Mr. T. P. Elli- son, Mr. Walter Keig, Mr. E. Braide, of the London and North-Western Railway Company, and others. The ab- sence of Mr. J. Fulasn Smith was apologized and accounted for by Mr, Potter. Mr. Smith had accompanied the party, as far as Liverpool, where after doing all in his power towards the comfort, he left them to attend to very important and pressing business. The luncheon was of afl excellent character, and at its termination toasts of " Ttw Queen and the rest of the Royal Family," " Our Guests," " Success to Missrs. Peter Walker and Sons. Warrington and Burton,""Ffealth of the Chairman"' were druuk.among the speakers being I Messts. Potter, Professor Grant, Alderman Francomb, W H. J. Jenkins, Rowe,Councillor Baird,Alderrnan Wright. The inner man satisfied, the boat was left and amusement of varied nature indulged in on shore. At Falcon Cliff the plea,ure of the visitors was admir- ably catered for by Professor Grant, admissions to the gardens being tree. The Warrington Volunteer Band was also in attendance,and played for dancing a portion of the time. Some of the parry visited Castletown,Rus- hen Castle, and the House of Keys, whilst others jour- neyed to Laxey and admired the wheel. The return journey was commenced at about 7 p.m , and another pleasant run was enjoyed, notwithstanding that the weather had cu,l•ii since morning. Liverpool was reached shortly before twelve o'clock, and in a com- paratively short space of time all had transferred them- selves to the train awaiting them at the Central Station. The journey from his point, when begun, was very short, it not being quite an hour after midnight when Warrington was reached safely reached by. all of the party. The trippers were also greatly indebted to Captain T. Gibson, of the " King Orry " for his careful attention and to the Chief steward, Mr. W. 11. Kelly for like con- sideration. •

THE EXAMIKK SATURDAY, JULY 13, 1895.

NEW BOOKS,MAGAZINES,ETC.

CASSELL AND Co.—A second issue Of " Cassell', Pocket Library "in " A White Baby," by James Welsh has been received by us. This little story is characterisec' chiefly by that pleasing simplicity which was such an admirable feature in " A King's Diary," though in " The White Baby "is also found simplicity of anothei sort, viz.—negro simplicity,e quality which for all that. is usually upheld by a strong under-current of honesty and faith. So it is in this story, the scene of which is pitched in the hone, and once hell, of the negro slave, the United States of Anserica. Pine Open, a small negre community in the story has for its minister a coloured man, the Rev. Abram White, who in addition to the mast unselfish labour on behalf of his parish has equal trust in his wife, notwithstanding that circumstance- occur which might justify a little doubt. The jealousy of a woman drives her to substitute her newly-born child (a white one) for the sable.hued specimen of humanity just arrived at the minister's house. From this point the "sword of fate " deals out acute self. punishment to the evil-doer by increasing love for her own child—now inaccessible—which this woman bears. Ultimately the clouds disperse and the mystery which had agitated Pine Open is solved, while the inno- cent and guilty causes of it disappear from the story. An amount of interest unexpected from the nature of the tale is furnished, and once started the reader is soon subeervient to a desire to reach the end.—" Little Folks " for July commences a new volume, and in it are also the first chapters of two new serials,one by Mr. Ed yard S. The latest edition of the Illustrated Standard Novels is" The Adventures of Hajji Baba of Ispahan," hy James J. Morier, with most natural illustrations by Mr. H. R Millar. The introduction is from the pen of the Hon. George Curzon, M.P., who says " llajji Baba at once became a favourite of the cultured reading public, and passed speedily through several editions. That popularity has never since been exhausted • and i the constant demand for a new issue 'is a prof not merely of the intrinsic merit of the book as acnntempor- ary portrait of Persian manners and life, hut also of the fidelity with which it continues to reflect, after the Nrise of three-quarters of a century, the salient and unchanging characteristics of a singularly unchanging oriental people." To the absolute truthfulness of the book Mr. Curzon, who is perfectly qualified to speak with authority, bears the following testimony :—" It is no frolic of imaeinative satire only ; it is a historical document. Toe figures that move across the stage are not pasteboard creations, but the living personalities disguised only in respect of their names, with whom Morier was brevight daily into contact while at Tehran. The majority of the incidents so skilfully woven into the narrative of the hero's adventures actually occurred, and can he identified by the student who is familiar with the ire-Meets of the time. It lathe ripened pro- duct of Morier'e Persian experience and reflections acquired and formed during the two visits he made to Persia in an official capacity."—" The Princess Mine," by Richard Harding Davies, is, a very odd conception, being based on a world-chase of this scion of royalty by Morton Carlton, the hero and artist, who has been to captivated with an illustration dereeting the Princess. as to lead him to follow her with half-rnrmed but hopeful intentions. WARRINGTON v. BROUGHTON.-It was to be regretted that the match between the above clubs should have had the unsatisfactory termination as was the case on Satur day. The match was the return, and in the original game, played at Broughton in May, it will be remem- bered that an unsatisfactory decision given by the Broughton umpire was averred by the Warringtonians to have spoiled their chances of victory. The same official acted on Saturday, and on several occasions during the progress of the game his decisions were severely ques- tioned, and the large crowd present were not slow at giving signs of disapprNval of such conduct. The climax was reached when a palpable case of Tomlinson being leg before wicket,was ruled in the batman's favour, and the home captain appealing to Mr. J. H. Payne to have the umpire substituted, the request was not acceded to, and the bell was rung and the players returned to the pavilion, the match thus being abandoned. Score and bowling analysis :- 1

THE GENERAL ELECTION.

A special supplement to the " London Gazette" was issued on Monday evening dissolving the Parliament elected in 1892, and ordering the issue of writs fir the calling together of a sew Parliament, which is to assem- ble on the 12 August. The writs for the London elections were delivered by special messengers on Monday evening and those for the elections in the provinces were handed in at the General Post-office in time for despatch by the evening mails. Mr. Gladstone's " watchwords " for the Liberal elec- tors are vindication of the right of the House of Com- mons to govern, and justice to Ireland. Mr. Morley in his address to the electors of Newcastle says that the question of the better government of Ire- land remains with hi m,as it has so long been, in the fore- front of Liberal policy, and it is on this issue that he makes his most direct appeal for a renewal of their con- fidence. add election MR. GLADSTONE AND THE HOUSE OF LORDS. The " Westminster Gazette " has received the follow leg message from Mr. Gladstone . " Hawarden, July 5, 1895. . .

"Above all other present purposes vinaicain the rights of the House of Commons as the organ of the nation ; and establish the honour of England, as well as Cool34ii- date the strength of the Empire, by conceding the just and constitutional claims of Ireland.

"W. E. G."

Mr. Beaufoy (Liberal) is being opposed by the friends of Local Veto at Kennington. Mr. Gladstone, having been asked to give his opinion, writes to the chairman of Mr. Beaufoy's Committee condemning a "practice which from time to time reappears in the Liberal party," but " which is carefully and wisely .voided by the Tories." Some section of the party, "anxious for some one cause out of many," determines to treat it as the sole cause at an election. The consequence is that, " While the Liberal party is the strongest in the country, small sec- tions of it, by most unwise and unintelligible conduct, convert it from a majority into a minority, and give power to those whose opinions they themselves usually detest and always disapprove." In a letter to a corres- pondent at Rochdale Mr. Gladstone says he can take no part in elections beyond giving his vote.

WARRINGTON.

WORKINGMHNS' GOSPAL I'EMPERINOR MISSION. On Saturday a pleasant social meeting was held in the hall in Buttermarket-street,when a conversa- tion took place upon the need for all Christian and temperance people being true to their principles, and working and voting only for candidates at the forthcoming election who pledged themselves to place the control of the liquor traffic in the hands of working people. On Sunday night a Gospel tem- perance meeting was held in the usual place, Bewsey-street. Mr. J. Drury presided, and several spirited addresses were delivered. SEWING MACHINES, Hand £2 18s. 6d. LOCAL PREACHERS MUTUAL AID ASSOCIATIO

During the course of Sunday, sermois were preached in Bold-st. and Bewsey-road, Penketh, Moore, Whitley, Antrobus and Appleton Wes'eyan Churches, and collections made on behalf of the Wesleyan Methodist Local Preachers' M utual Aid Association. This association was established in the year 184 s to afford relief to its members during periods of sickness and in old age, and also to pro- vide a sum of money for their decent interment. It now numbers 5,328 benefit members and 2,024 honorary members. We understand that the total amount paid to the sick, seed, and for deaths since the association commenced payment to the mem- bers in 1851 has been as follows: To Ock members. .E 46,764 to the superannuated, £75311; and for deaths, £16,175. Sermons for the same object will be preached in other Wesleyan churches to-morrow

ACCIDENTS.

On Tuesday a lad named Thomas Rowlinson,nine. years of age, and living at 15, Lord Nelson-st., was admitted into the Infirmary suffering from a scalp wound and a crushed arm. It appears that Row- linson was playing in the roadway near his home when he was knocked down by a lurry. He was quickly removed to the Infirmary for treatment.— On Monday night, a serious accident occurred at Hallam Forge. A man named John Carroll (40). and living in Portland-st., was following his usual employment when he was severely scalded on his feet and legs. He was quickly removed to his home, and a doctor summoned, who ordered Carroll to the Infirmary, where he was detained.

FORTHCOMING VISIT OF TM/ nAItOR AND CORPORA-

TION TO THE CLIO.

The annual deputation of the School Attendance Committee to the Ship Clio, will be held on July 19. Occasion is taken of the visit to hand over to the superintendent a cheque on behalf of the Corpora- tion. It is understood that the Mayor has also invited the whole of the councillors to Join him in the visit this year, which will take place on the 19th of July. Subsequent to the inspection of the Clio, the Corporation will dine with the Mayor at Bangor, from whence they will afterwards return home by train.
