APPLETON WATER SUPPLY.

Mr. Hall, of Ivy Bank, Appleton, wrote stating that he had a great amount of sympathy for tbe inhabitants of Weston who were afflicted with a deficient water sup- ply. His sympathy was mainly created in consequence of his being a fellow sufferer—in fact there were the in- habitants of 24 houses suffering likewise. He trusted the Westooians might have a much more speedy deliver- ance from their troubles than appeared probable for them. The Council would remember Mr. Robert Davies brought the subject before them twelve months ago. What had they done towards giving them a continuous supply of water in the:meantime ? They had had a whole year to do something. To-day they were in a worse position than they were at that time.Ele almost wished the whole of the members of the Council resided where they were led to depend on a town's supply, and then the supply daily withheld from them. Seeing that they had been long suffering and had patiently borne their afflictions for twelve months, he might say that for several years there had been a shrinkage in the supply year by year, until it had become so small that it was unbearable. It was time some steps were taken by the Authority towards providing them with something like a continuous supply. Now, and for some time, their supply was only by night. The most frequent hour for making its appearance was 9 30—he had seen it 11.30 and it took its departure not later than six a.m., but often earlier. This went on daily, or rather nightly, so that unless they kept late hours or arose early they were without water for the whole day. This was no light matter for those who had to endure it daily, and the time had arrived when the Authority ought to take up the question generally of a water supply to the higher parts of the district, and especially for Apple- ton. He did not say this selfishly, but they knew that Appleton had the first claim for water on the Water Company, till the Authority negligently, he would not say wantonly, allowed the Water Company to obliterate it by a clause in a more recent Act.— In answer to a question the Clerk said that if the War- rington Water Works did not supply Appleton properly, the Council could give tt em notice that unless they were able and willing to supply the inhabitants, they would do it themselves.—Mr. Darbyshire said he would like to k. ow whether the writer had communicated with the local Parish Council —Mr. Davenport : Yes several times.—Mr. Darbyshire said that it was not good for two lor three to be poking at this question from different standpoints.—Mr. Davenport said there had been a deal of correspondence with the Corporation of Warrington with reference to the water supply being insufficient. They could not get anything satisfactory from them.— Mr. Parr was of the opinion that according to the Act, the Corporation were obliged to fiod Appleton with a sufficient supply of water.—The Clerk was not sure that they were. The Council could do as he had previously pointed out.—The Chairman said it was perhaps advis- able to write to the Corporation of Warrington asking whether they were able and willing to supply Appleton with water.—The Clerk said several letters had been written to them and the Corporation had promised to provide a better supply.—Mr. Brandreth thought the Council should have a recommendation from the town- ship. He moved that the Clerk write to the Chairman of the Parish Council of Appleton asking him to call the residents together to consider the question and make re- commendations as to what the Council should do in the matter.—Mr. Darbyshire seconded the proposition,which was carried.

===== 0002601_18950713_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
WESTON WATER SUPPLY AGAIN.

A communication from Mr. M4rshall, secretary of the Runcorn Urban District Council,was read with reference to the water supply of Weston. He said "At the last meeting of the Water Committee they gave their officers instructions that as soon as this report came to hand they must inquire into the alleged defects and make good those which might be discovered for which this author- ity is responsible. I may say that with regard to the houses between the Round-house and the Royal Oak Inn a defect was discovered before the report came to hand. and which has been put right. This will give some of the houses a better supply. A thorough inspection will be made with regard to the other houses named, and you may rest assured that we shall do all we can to put matters on a satisfactory basis with regard to the 45 houses which your inspector reports as being entirely without water, I beg to inform you that on looking up our men's time sheets we find that the water was turned off at the time of Mr. Farrington's visit, to enable a con- nection to be made from the main to Beaconsfield House, Weston Point. Jam sorry that your Inspector had the trouble of visiting these houses when the water happened to be turned off. I have mentioned the matter to my chairman and he wishes me to ask if your Authority will be good enough to instruct your Inspector to again visit the 45 houses in question and then advim3 us as to how he finds the water supply on his second visit. Kindly let me have this information as soon as possible, as my committee is anxious to di all they can to give as good a supply to Weston as possible and as soon as possible."— Mr. Aaron said the statement in the letter that the water was turned off at the time of the Inspector's visit was inaccurate. He contended that the supply was still intermittent, and last Saturday they had not a "tooth- full." It had, however,- been a little better lately.—Mr. Farrington was instructed to make a second visit and report.

===== 0002601_18950713_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
A DANGEROUS APPROACH AT WANON INFERIOR.

Regarding the dangerous state of the approaches from the Chester Main-road to the new road leading over the Ship Canal at the swing bridge. Walton Inferior, the committee appointed reported tP•at the banks at the west side being high and the corners from the Chester Main-road being sharp it was impossible to see the traffic coming in a contrary iirection. It was therefore very dangerous in its present condition. The committee recommended that as the landowner, Sir Gilbert Green- all;Bart.,was willing to give the land to widen the Ches- ter-road, the particulars should be forwarded to the Cheshire County Council, asking them to give instruc- tions to their Surveyor to take the matter promptly in hand, and have the requisite improvements done.—This was agreed to.

===== 0002601_18950713_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BRIDLE ROAD AT ANTROBIJS.

The committee appointed to consider this matter re- ported that they were unanimously agreed that an en- croachment had been made on the public rights—in the first place by Mr. Richard Vernon, formerly a tenasit on one of Sir E. Antrobus's farms ; and in the second place by Mr. Joseph Bolshaw, on and adjoining the road. The committee recommended the Council to request Sir Edmund Antrobus to put up a gate suitable for a bridle road in place of the fenced rails to the satisfaction of the Council or their Surveyor.—The report of the com- mittee was adopted.

===== 0002601_18950713_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
A FLOODED HIGHWAY AT THELWALL.

The Surveyor reported that the public highway under the Bridgewater Canal and the London and North- Western Railway bridges at Thrlwall was frequently flooded. He had comnnunieated with both companies and both disclaimed any fault in the matter. The road was impassable at times which *as a serious matter.—lt was resolved on the motion of Mr. Whitlow, seconded by Mr. Davenport, that the Clerk write to the late sur- veyor of the Bueldow Highway Board asking for in- formation as to what was done on former occasions.

TRE MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. Adams report that scarlet fever had occurred at Moore. There were two cases of scarlet fever at Weston Point, and measles were prevalent at Weston.—Mr. Chadwick said he had been asked by the Rev. W. H. S,ables to apply for permission to have the day schools closed at Weston on account of the prevalent disease.— Dr. Adams said it was questionable whether they should interfere with the requirementsof the Education Depart- ment. He thought it would I.‘s wiser to wait to see if any fresh cases developed. There were four families aff-cted with measles, with twelve children.—Mr. Chad- wick sai3 the examination of the children was over and it would only be a prolonged holiday.—Dr. Adams said Weston had been particularly unfortunate. It had scarcely been free from scarlet fever during the past twelve months, and it seemed a pity if they were now to be inflicted with an epidemic of measles.—Mr. Asron moved that Dr. Adams visit Weston, and if he thought it advisable to close the se.haols to do so.—An amendment was moved hv Mr. Darbrshire that there was not sufficient information to justify the Council in closing the schools.—On being put to the vote Mr. Aaron's resolution was carried. ANOTHER SHIP CANAL COMPLAINT. Mr. Parr submitted A. letter from Mr. T. Hall. who stated that the Ship Canal Co. were turning the "Stag" swing bridge at Walton for the purpose of passifig bal- last trains underneath This was contrary to the powers of the company.—Mr. Pim. sail ,he Canal Company had no right to stop the traffic for that purpose at all. The late Daresbury Highway Bond refused them permission. —lt was ultimately decided that a small committee con- sisting of Messtirs 13,trr,Weir.and Davenport should visit the place and report to the a onneil. THE INSPECTOR'S REPORT. Mr. Farrington. nuia. PCP iesnector. reported that the town pit at Thelwall was pollute.l with sewage. It was explained by Admiral Parry that the matter would be brought before the nex, Parish Council meeting.—The Inspector reported th,t 15 houses in the township of Grappenhall were wit hnnt a water supply.—lt was de- cided that notice he served ipon the owners for a supply to be provided.—He also reported that five houses situ- ated at Belsbv were without a water supply.--Notices were ordered to be served noon the owners.—He further reported that three houses in Helsby were without Suffi- dent ventilation and there vas also itsufficient privy accommod,tim. —lt was d‘e;dsd that the matter be re ferred to the Medical Officer of Health for his report therenn.—A certificate of occupation was granted for a caretaker's how, near b. the Wesleyan Chanel, Five Crosses, Frodshem This concluded the business.

MESSRS. W. A. BISHOP AND CO., AND THE CORPORATION.

THE WHARF QUESTION.

In the discussions which have taken place in the Town Council upon the recommendation of the Rivers' Committee to let a piece of land near the river to Messrs. W. A. Bishop and Company, for the construction of a wharf, the correspondence with different authorities concerned has been frequently referred to,SI essrs. Bishop and Company have there- fore supplied us with copies of the following letters which show that the proposed wharf has been sanc- tioned by all these outside authorities, including the Ship Canal Company. The following are the letters :

- `• Mersey Conservancy, 5, King-st., Westminster, 16th November, 1894. Gentlemen,—With reference to your letter of the 10th inst. to Lieutenant Sampson, I have to acquaint you that I have not any objection to offer on the part of this Conservancy to the wharf which von propose to construct on the Lancashire side of the Mer- sey above Warrington Bridge, in accordance with the plans which I return to you herewith.—Yours faithfully, GEORGE HENRY RICHARDS, Admiral,Acting Conservator. Messrs. W. A. Bishop and Co." "Manchester Ship Canal Co., Secretary's Office,Spring Gardens, Manchester, 20th March, 1895. Dear Sir,—l am informed by Messrs. Fraser and Warburton that your Corporation are the owners of the land upon which Messrs. Bishop and Co. propose to erect a wharf on the river, and, assuming that this is the case, I have to state that there is no objection on the part of this company to the erection of such wharf in accordance with the plans which they have submitted to us.—Yours faith- fully, A. H. WHITWORTH, Secretary. J. Lyon Whittle, Eq., Town Clerk, Warrington." Duchy of Lancaster, 14th December, 1894. Gentle- men,—The Surveyor General of the Duchy having re- ported upon your application of the 9th ult. lam to inform you that, upon the assumption that the land marked on the plan you send, " proposed site for wharf," belongs to your company in fee, the Chancellor of the Duchy is prepared to grant to your Company by her Majesty's Right and Interest, except the righ., to minerals, in and to so much of the foreshore of the river Mersey as is coloured rod on the plan hereto annexed, upon payment of the sum of £4O by way of considera- tion. The grant would be prepared in the Duchy offices and would be in the form usually adopted for such grants and the fees of office and tue stamp duty thereon amounting to £5 5s would have to be paid by your company before the Duchy seal is affixed. Upon your formally accepting these terms a draft shall be prepared and forwarded for your perusal.-4 am, your obedient servant. J. GARDENER D. ENGLEHEART. Messrs. W. A. Bishop and Co." The Chancellor, Duchy of Lancaster, London, E.G.— Sir,—ln reply to your letter of the 14th ult., we have been in correspondence with the Town Clerk of War- rington on the subject of the proposed site for wharf. The land marked on the plan does not belong to us, but is claimed by the Corporation of Warrington on the ground that they made the street which runs by the side of it. This being so we thought it hest that the Corporation should pay you the sum of £4O by the way of consideration, together with the fees and office duty. We return the plan,and would be glad if you will inform us whether you could not arrange the matter with the Warrington Corporation.—Yours faithfully, W. A. BISHOP & CO."

The last letter, it will be seen, confirms ' ite statement made in the Town Council that it was mainly through the action of Messrs. Bishop and Co. that toe Corporation acquired the foreshore upon which it was proposed to erect the wharf, and Mr. Fleming, the representative of Messrs. Bishop and Co.,in Warrington, informed a representative of the" Examiner "that there had been eight months' labour in correspondence and the preparation of plans.

otify.

ALLEGED BREACH OF CONTRACT.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, week before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes,John F. Dixon, Arpley-street, Warrington, sued Messrs. Riley and Sons, Hapton, near Accrington, chemical manufacturers, for 184. 2d. for damages for alleged breach of contract, and also for the carriage of goods to and from Bradford. Mr. F. R. Roberts, M.A., L.L.8., solicitor, Warrington, appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Nowell, solicitor, Burnley, for the defendant. _ .

Mr. Roberts, in opening the case for the plaintiff, said that the article supplied was muriate of ammonia for galvanising purposes. The claim was, for loss of profit, £3 is. 4d., and for carriage to and from Bradford, £3 1.6 i 10.1. Ott March of the present year, the defendants wrote to theplaintiff for a sample oftmuriate of ammonia. Samples of two qualitities were sent, andia short time afterwards Messrs. Riley and Company directed two tons to be sent of the best quality. Plaintiff at once de- livered three casks of the muriate which were supplied by Mr. Tinnion,of Messrs. Kenyon and Co. as part of the order, and on April Ist,he sent the remainder. No com- plaint was made until April sth. when a letter was re- ceived from the defendants stating that the muriate was heavy with moisture. If that were the case, which the plaintiff did not admit, the moisture must have been absorbed during the course of transit to Bradford, which was its destination, it being required by Messrs. Water- house and Company. The letter also said that the latter refused to accept it. On April 9ch, a letter was received informimg the plaintiff that the muriate was lying at Bradford station at plaintiff's own risk. The plaintiff upon'that took back the goods under protest. The plain- tiff sent in his bill which the defendants refused to pay. He took the goods back because muriate of ammonia was a very easily damaged article, and if allowed to stand on the station long, might have been seriously damaged. He had every confidence that the quality was good,and thii was shown by the fact that the manu- facturer had paid hini back the price that he had charged him. On the every day the goods were returned the manufacturer had despatched a portion of them to another customer, and no complaints had been made. The letter which Mr. Dixon sent on taking back the goods, was then read, but the judge held that there was no protest, and that there was no breach of contract. The only claim which could be made was for expenses. . . . . .

- After consulting with his client, Mr. hoberts with- drew the claim for breach of contract.

Francis Dixon was then called. He said that he was manager for the plaintiff who was his son, and was a chemical merchant. He obtained the samples which were sent to Messrs. Riley from Messrs. Kenyon, the manufacturers. The bulk sent to the defendants was equal to the sample. David Tinnion, managing partner for Messrs. Kenyon, chemical manufacturers, Bank Q:iay, said that he had been connected with the firm for 18 yeirs. When asked for a sample by Mr. Dixcn, he took it from the stock, and the muriate sent out was fully equal, in fact better than the sample. It was better because at the time the order was sent off, the muriate was not so dark in colour as at the time the sample was sent. They never guaranteed the quality of the muriate they sent out. It always schrank when kept in casks.—Cross-examined : He simply took a sample from the stock in the works. He did not keep any to compare with the sample sent. An average quality was sent with the order. Mr. Nowell briefly addressed the Court for the de- fence and then called upon Frank English, who said that he was a chemist in the service of the defendant. He received the muriate in a bottle produced as a sample from Mr. Dixon, and also the other bottle produced from the order which was supplied. The article supplied was damp,and not equal to the sample.—Cross-examined : He did not take the sample himself, but received it from Herbert Spinks,the traveller for the Company. ' Herbert Spinks said that he received an order from Messrs. Waterhouse Bros., Bradford, for muriate of am- monia for galvanising purposes, and he ordered it from Mr. Dixon. He saw the casks when they arrived, and they were in a wet icondition. In one cask which was opened, and out of which the sample produced had been obtained, the muriate had sunk four or five inches, and the water had drained out of them.—Cross•examined : He did not analyse the water,and could not tell whether there was any muriate in the water which drained from the casks. _ . .

His Honour said that the stare of the casks when they arrived at their destination was of no importance. The defendant should prove that it was in the same condi- tion when sent from Warrin4ton. Mr. Nowell called two more witnesses, Fred Green- wood, goods agent, Dudley Hill station, and Edward Waterhouse, of the firm to which the muriate was CON- signed, but as neither these gentlemen could prove the condition of the goods when consigned, his Honour said that he must give judgment for the plaintiff for the ex- penses of carriage. He had no evidence before him that the goods were in an unsound state when sent from Warrington. lie would therefore give judgment for plaintiff for £3 les. 10d.

The decision of the Court of Appeal in a Bettwx-y-Coed case in which damages were claimed for injury sustained through the lettir.g of apartments in which there was infectious disease was given on Tuesday. The judgment of the Court was,that the landlord of a house is not responsi- ble for infection which he has neither knowledge of nor power to prevent, such, for instance, as the visit to the house of an infected person who might leave the infection behind him.

TOWN COUNCIL NOTES.

The Rivers Committee must by this time have come to the conclusion that it is by far the most important of the Committees of the Town Council, for at the last meeting for the third time it mono- polised the lion's share ot the discussion. The other Committees were nowhere, in comparison—at least scarcely anywhere. The Health Committee was reminded that there are two or three serious nui- sances which are causing a good deal of discomfort tu a portion of the inhabitants of Warrington, and which require immediate attention. One of these is the stagnant portion of old river behind Wilders- pool Causeway which, it was said, had become so bad as to drive tenants from some of the adjacent houses. The other is Sankey brook—the very name of which seems to be associated with unpleasant odours. Councillor Plumpton drew a lively picture of the present state of the brook, and Councillor Monks ventured the remark that if an aldertnan or two had lived in the Bank Quay direction this par- ticular nuisance would have received more attention than it has done.

The Fire Engine Committee also came in for lertain amount of criticism. The Committee pro- posed to augment the fire brigade by six men and to plovide certain aldiion& appliances, but it was suggested that nothing of this kind should be done till the special committee appointed to inquire in- to the affiirs of the brigade had made its reports, and most of the minutes of the committee were withdrawn. Enough was said in the debate to show that there is plenty of room for inquiry by this Committee. One speaker, Councillor Plumpton, stated that hydrants had been put down by the Water Committee, but no marks had been made to indicate their position; and Councillor Roberts as- , serted that there had been a considerable amount of ' friction between the Water Committee and the Fire Engine Committee 'Nis was denied by Alderman Francomh, the chairman of the first-named com- mittee, but other members of the Council supported the allegation of Councillor Roberts. It was fur- ther alleged that even the Water Committee did not themseives'know where all the hydrants were, and that there were two, if not three, different sorts of hydrants in the town. Altogether the debate was not at all of a reasr.uring nature with regard to the arrangements made for coping with fire, and, as we have said, there is clearly room for inquiry by the special committee. Meanwhile, the Fire Engine Committee is as Councillor Woods said with un- premeditated humour, in a state of' superstition." 1

The minutes of the Rivers Committee upon which the discussion turned were the same as those re- jected at the June meeting of the Council. One I proposal referred to the letting to Messrs. Bishop I and Co., a plot of land near the river for the erec- tion of a wharf, and in moving the adoption of this proposal Councillor Roberts expressed the opinion that the previous rejection had been largely due to a misapprehension of a personal nature Ile believed that certain councillors had been led to believe that some members of the Rivers Committee who were connected with a newly-formed shipping company were personally interested in the construction of the wharf. This, Mr. Roberts pointed out, was a complete mistake as the ships owned by the Company were sea-going vessels and could not pos- sibly ger, up to the wharf. ilaving brushed this cob- web aside, and also the fallacious argument that the Ship Canal Company were opposed to the wharf, Mr. Roberts gave some reasons why the , Council should allow the wharf to be constructed He thought it was the duty of the Council to offer all the facilities they could for the encouragement ,of local industries. The objects of Messrs. Bishop and Co. who had established a new industry in the town, was to get their materials to and from their , works in a less expensive way than at present, and , if in the future they undertook a certain amount of I general carrying that would be of benefit to the smaller traders in the town. That the Corporation were making a Roori bargain be proved by showing that they would get a rent of £2O a year for a piece of land which had only cost them £4O, and which I they had secured mainly through the instrumenta- lity of Messrs. Bishop and Ce.

This was the case of the Committee so far as the wharf was concerned, and it seemed conclusive enough. But it was soon evident that the opposi- tion was newly, if not, quite, as strong as ever. Councillor El.pherd again moved an amendment to reject the proposal but studiously refrained on this occasion from giving any reason for his action. The argument that the wharf would ,be injurious to the Ship Cana) had evidently become unusable, Alder- man Greenall siconded the amendment. but Coun- cillor Plumpton, who:was neutral on the previous occasion, declared that he would !support the Committee on this CouncillAr R. T. Fairclough urged that an obstruction to the street would be caused by the when, and Alderman Sutton ingen- iously suggested that the wharf question should be allowed to rest until the relative positions of the Corporation and the Ship Canal Company with re- gard to the river had been definitely ascertained. Councillor Monks warmly repudiated the insinua- tions that he was likely to receive pecuniary benefit from the wharf.as he had not even any con- nection with the shipping company that, had been ' named. He was in favour of the wharf because at the present time the small traders and shopkeepers were not able to derive any advantage from the river. As for the large manufacturers they looked after themselves. Councillor Monks was also sar- castic with regard to the settling of public busi- ness in the bar parlours of public-houses and at political clubs. Another convert to the reasonable- ness of the committee's proposal was Councillor Longton, hut Councillor Woods had nut changed his mind. He was still dead against the wharf,and be waxed indignant at Councillor Monk's reference to bar parlours and political clubs. The committee were supported by Councillors Green and Bennett; but on a vote being taken the amendment was again carried, this time II a majority of sixteen to ten. The same fate awaited the recommendation of the Rivers Committee with respect to its representa- tion on the Upper Mersey Navigation Commission, the majority for the amendment this time being twelve to nine.

_ WALKING DAY BANDS. Sir.—ln your iSF4'3I of last Saturday the 29th het in the report of the Writhing Day Procession you. General Post Office, Warrington, July 4tti,1895.

" WARRINGTON ASSESSMENTS.' Sir,—/Lay I ask the indulgence for the insertion of this letter in Four fair and uninfleenced journal. It is a well known fact, that the" doings and resets" of the meet- ings of your municipal Council and Boards of Guardians are to people living outside your noted borough an- xiously looked for through the Press. It is well within your recollection resided in your " fortressed borough of tied Tints" for a very short time(having heels success- ful in ,dofesiting one of your local mighty brewers in the 'tendering of a-higher rental to your Corporation for one of their licensed properties.) I will not here relate the impediments and unusual restrictions placed on me be- fore I could get possession of the "pub" I so success- fully tendered for. Before I could enter into possession busybodies in and out of the Council and members of Boards of •Guardians w( re anxious to know if my agree- ment was signed and settled as to the conditions of the lease of the pub. The then Assessment Committee were greatly interested in making enquiries from the Town Clerk and needless comments upon me. However, the assessment stood of the Nag's Head on a rental of £2OO at £ll3. I gave EV more of a rental—yet it brought from my friends (1) of the committee the ter- rible increase of £152," besides the previous assessment of £111." HOW so strange and enormous an increase ? I had not more premises! Or was it because I defeated local brewers? Oh no that was not the reason. But to use the words of a Surveyor of Taxes " It is not easy to know what rental those brewers do pay! " Well my rent could be very easy to know. But I did not satisfy my friends by paying their assessment. Will any fear- less ratepayer ask their representative on the Council , the income arising from the Queens' Hotel on the moneys of the ratepayers from October, 1893 to the present date, considering law charges, allowance of rentals (two occasions) and if any profit can be shown on the rate- payers' money? My occupancy was short, but it is a consolation to know I entered your borough by defeating a brewer, also your Corporation and Assessment Com- mittee in their tug-of-war contest. Therefore I ask, has the licensed property purchased by the Corporation with the ratepayers' money been profitable ?—Yours truly, LAURENCE FLOOOD.

Dublin, June 26th, 1895.

HOW IRISHMEN AND CATHOLICS SHOULD VOTE.

Sir,--its an Irishman and a Catholic I should like to warn my fellow-countrymen and co-religionists in this oonstituency against being led away or influenced by the charaCteristieTory electioneering dodge of placarding the hoardings around with alleged extracts from Catholic biahops,respecting the education question,and making it in a measure, appear that the aforesaid bishops had ordered, under the pain ot excommunication, the return of Mr. Pierpoint. Now,this is entirely misleading,and is but a pitiful political "move." The bishops' mauifesto refers to matters respecting education which are too intricate to be considered in detail now,and says nothing about Tories or Liberals as such. The great matter for Irishmen and Catholics to con- sider, in this regard,is whether the party represented by Salisbury and Balfour, or that represented by Glad- stone and JchnMorley. is more nicely to treat with favour and considerateness any question or matter, whether religious or secular, affecting Irishmen and Catholics. Salisbury openly and andisavowedly refers to and con- siders the Irish (and inferentially Catholic) as savages and" Hottentots," and only fit for " perpetual coer- cion" and slavery ; and his relative, Balfour, during his regime, showed his consideration for Irishmen. and especially Catholics, by imprisoning priests wholesale, for the technical offence of making political speeches, kc., and subjecting them to the degradation of herding with pickpockets and garroters! We all know how very differently "honest John Morley" has governed Ireland during the past few years : and, all things con- sidered, I say it would he the basest ingratitude as well as the most suicidal folly for any Irishman or Catholic to vote, under almost any circumstances, for a Tory. I would much sooner believe in the natural impossibil- ity or" miracle " of the " leopard changing his spots " or the mountains changing places with the valleys, than in the probability of the Tories treating Irishmen with more consideration than Liberals would. Tories may make promises.but they can break them as readily as a hungry man would a pie-crust. I therefore hope that no Irish- man or Irish Catholic in this town will be guilty of the ingratitude and folly of voting for a Tory.—Yours faithfully,

THE HEAWSE 0' LORDS.

BY JOHN WALKIII.

Owd Caleb wer a rum owd cwt; A s'unner, too, for werkin ; He'd always say just wod he thowt, When he wer on for le:kin. He kept a breyd o' garden greawnd, An' one day he wer toilin— To widen th' garden gate a bit— Till he wer welly boilin. A lump o' rock stood e'en i'th' way; He're sweerin' like a trooper. When who should come up, smush and gay. But th' Tory Squire Cooper. "Swearing," the Squire said, " is a sin, And anger it increases; You bore a hole, put powder in, And rend the reek in pieces." "That's good advice," Owd Caleb said ; " Id'll make id fly like winkin ; But this is wod popt in my head, . An set me sooa a-thinkin ; Aw thowt thad rock, like th' Heawse of L-irds, Wet verra ill to fashion ; An when aw said them tooathri words— Id put me in a passion. But eh ! thad Heawse has bed id day, There's beawn to be a ruction; We're going to widen th' owd gateway— We'll ha' no moors obst uction. Thad Heawse 'll hey to gcoa, egad ! We'll bang at till wi brast id ; Or else we'll try thy tip. mi lad— We winnot sweer ; blast id." 4 COPYRIGHT.

BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN_ METHODIST CHURCH :

CHOIR TRIP.

The annual trip of the choir of Bald; street Wesleyan Church was held on Monday, the place which was chosen being Chester. The party, numbering about 22, left Bank Quay by the 95 train, and reached their destination at 10.5. The weather was all that could be desired, and after visiting the cathedral, the ancient walls, and other places of interest, the company partook of dinner at the Brook-street refreshment rooms. In the afternoon arrangements were made with Mr. Capner, late of Warrington, for a trip on his fine steamer, the Ontario, to Grosvenor Bridge, which was very much enjoyed. At tea the pleasure of the party was very much increased by the presence of some who were unable to be present earlier in the day. Mr. Harrison, organist and choirmaster, with his usual kindness, under- took the whole ot the arrangements, and great praise is due to him for the admirable way in which they, were carried out. The return journey Was made by the 8.30 train, home being reached soon after nine, and thus ended what all agreed to be one of the most enjoyable days ever spent by the choir. The trustees of the church very kindly made the arrangements whereby the trip was held.

TILE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 13, 1895

RUNCORN AND WIDNES. INEWr'ZON DIVISION ELECTION.

Torn BY A SAILOR AT RUNCORN. NEWTON DIVISION. At Runcorn Police Court on Friday, before Messrs. T. Handley and E. Imison Frankuourt, aged 18, belonging ~Ate, campaign in this district is being pushed for- to Watchet. Somersetshire, and cook on the schooner "lard with great vigour. Mr. Moon, Liberal candi- J.W.V., lying at Runcorn docks, was charged with steal- ttate,, is doing all in his power to make himself ing £3, the money of John Norman, captain of t' Ircrown, and to lay his views before the co-nstitu- schooner in question.—The prisoner admitted ha • ents. Already several meetings have been taken the money. After evidence by John Norma- .4`,;:ji held in the outside districts, and so far P.C. Ells, the prisoner was committed to gaol ,e,no he has been well received, especially amongst the month. colliers, with whom his views on the labour ques- tion are quite in accord. Mr. Walkden, the miner's agent, has so far rendered valuable assistance, and the plain spoken manner in which he appeals to the miners, carries much weight. The prospects of the Liberal party on this occasion are certainly very bright, and it is expected that the Conserva- tive majority of 894 will be reduced, if not altogether extinguished.. The rooms engaged by the Liberal agent, Mr. Renshaw, are centrally situated, and excellently adapted to the requirements. There is a host of willing workers on the spot, and it certainly speaks' well for the energy eh the agent, when it is stated' that Mr. Moon's address was reported all over he division fully a week before that of Mr. Legh, and the various kinds of literature are already in the bands of many of the electors. Some petty spite has been displayed already in connection with the contest. Early one morning people were surprised to find the addresses of Mr. Moon which were .posted on hoardings besmeared with mud, thus rendering ' their perusal an impossibility. This is no child's work, and judging from the manner in whick the smearing has been done, it has been executed by one hand, and in a systematic style. The work is contemptible, and only shows the fear some people labour under, that such an enlightened address may be the means of securing converts to the cause. It is a curious coincidence that not one of Mr. legh's addresses have been smudged, and the inference to be drawn is obvious. The date of nomination has been fixed for Mon- day next, and the polling on the Monday fallowing, viz., July f'2nd. According to present arrange- ments the result of the poll will not be declared until next day. This is a departure from the usual procedure, and will cause a large amount of disap- pointment and grumbling. There is no apparent reason why the poll should net be declared the same night, and it is to be hoped that the decision is not final.

Ba7NCORST BOARD OF GUARDIANE. meeting of this Board was held at Dutton Work: house on Tuesday. Mr. J. Knowles occupied the chair and there were present in addition to the members of the Runcorn RuratCouncil, Bev. Dr. GaStaldi, Messrs. Thomas Handley, J.P., S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., A. R. Norman T. Lea, S. Yarwood, and Mr. J. Ashton (clerk), Mr. J. Norman, (deputy clerk), and Mr. Cunnah (work- house master).—A communication was read from the Mansfield Union stating that the recent conference on the treatment of casuals with the president of the Local Government Board was so unsatisfactory to the Guardians of the Union that they had determined to ask every Poor Law Union in the country to petition the Board to institute a searching enquiry into the present treatment and relief of casuals with a view to se amendment of the law. The Runcorn Board were asked to adopt and petition on the subject.—Mr. Handley said he did not think it affected their Union very much. The increase in the number of casuals was very little in their district.—Dr. Gastaldi proposed that the communi- cation lie upon the table.—Mr. Darbyshire said the sub- ject was a very ,important one, although it did not affect their Union very much. It affected the country at large.—Mr. Handley thought it would be better to know something more about it before dealing with the matter.—The Workhouse-master in answer to a question said the casuals calling at their workhouse did not aver- age above three or four a night. The matter was ultim- ately referred to the House Committee.—This concluded the business

LETTER FROM MR. GLADSTONE TO MR. MOON. Mr. Moon, the Liberal candidate for Newton has re- ceived the following communication from Mr. Glad- stone

--„ . Hawarden, July 4. Dear sir,--I write in haste to avoid further delay amidst a chaos of business. Cordially wishing you sue- cess.—l remain, yours very faithfully, W. R. GLADSTONH.

THE REV. T. BRACKEN/II:MY AND THE UNE OF THE FRANCHISE.

Preaching in St. P,:ul'73-iFelie-ian Church, Runcorn, on Sunday evening, the Rev. T. Brackenbury said Eng- lish electors would have a splendid opportunity for the exercise of Christian patriotism in the course of a few days. More than 850 members of Parliament would be chosen, and they would be invested with legislative , power, for good or for evil, and their influence would leffect the destiny of more than 300,000,000 of souls, and the future of England for centuries to come. Every heusehoulder in the country had now before him the question " Whom shall I choose to make laws for me and my house ?" They thought the pulpit should have nothing to do with party politics, and of this he intended to keep clear. At the same time, in a crisis like the pre- sent, every pulpit should have a message for tLe voter. Every elector should be reminded that the right to vote was a responsibility as well as a privilege, aud that the use he made of his franchise would be a matter of in- quiry at the judgment seat of Christ. The franchise should be used in an unselfish and disinterested spirit, and should be exercised with an enlightened conscien- tiousness. If they would vote conscientiously they must not put their conscience in the keeping of any single man or body of men. If they would be patrio- tic and Christian electors, they must so exercise the franchise as to have a conscience void of offence to- wards God and towards men. It was to be used under a strong sense of religious responsibility, and he pointed out that the question the House of Commons had to dis- cuss and decide involved the most important moral and religious issues. PRESENTATION TO COLOUR-SERGEANT PLATER.

At the Volunteer Drill Hall, on Friday evening, Colour- Sergeant Player, late instructor to the detachment, was presented with a handsome silver tea service. The inscription ran as follows " Presented to W. Player, late sergeant-instructor of F and G Companies of the Earl of Cheater's Regiment, by the officers, non-commis- sioned officers, men, past and present, of the Runcorn detachment as a mark of goodwill and esteem." In malcißg the presentation Captain Ashton said it afforded him great pleasure indeed to make the presentation on behalf of the detachment, as a mark of their esteem for his services, eeending over 19 years. He was very pleased to he able to tell their late instructor that it had been subscribed for by members of the detachrnent,both past and present, and contributions had been received from the other instructors in the battalion. He had only had the pleasure of serving a comparatively short period during his instructorship, but he had always found him most anxious to keep up the dignity of the corps, and in every way to efficiently fulfil the duties of the office. With respect to the recruits he noticed that he was most ae careful in their training, and turned them over to th companies well advanced in efficiency. Captain Ashton hoped that in years to come Colour-sergeant Piayer,when he looked hack upon the services with the Runc3rn Vol- unteers, would do so with as much pleasure as did the members of the detachment. The band then played " Auld Lang Syne."—Colour-Sergeant Player sincerely thanked the subscribers for their kindness in presenting to him such a handsome girt. He would treasure it vi-ry highly'. He had always endeavoured to do his duty,and he was delighted that his services had met with their appreciation.

THINGS HEAPD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

Now that we are in for a "fight " it behoves every true Liberal to step into the electioneering ranks and carry the Liberal flag in triumph once more. The Liberals in the Northwi?,h division, and especially those in the Runcorn portion of it, hare a brilliant record which ron,t he maintained, and it is only cock-suredness on their part which consti- tutes a real danger to the accomplishment of this end. Sir John Brunner's opponent, Mr. Thomas Ward, was only decided upon at the last moment —in fnct, he seems to have consented on the grounds that he thought there should he a contest. He felt, so he said, that they had been driven into a 'corner Itt, the last moment. with hardly time to turn round, and in consequence he decided to step into the breach. The Conservatives will, no doubt, think it very kind and courageous of him to do so, but his chances of winning the seat are very re- mote it the result of the Liberal canvass—so far as it has gone—is anything toga by. There may per- haps be some little difficulty in getting it expressed on the ballot paper for there is generally a certain coolness on the part of some in putting in an ap- pearance, when the sole object seems to resolve it- self into piling on a big majority. This has been the cause of many a defeat and it is the duty of every Liberal to work and vote as though the re- sult depended upon him.

That the division is a mast important one may be judged from the fact that it contains 11.788 electors and no fewer than 16 polling districts. The nominatioa day will be on the 19th inst., and the contest is fixed for Wednesday, the 24th inst.

At this print it may not be uninterestinz to re- fer to the flaures at the last election. On that oc- casion Mr. Brunner polled 5,580, and Mr. Whiteley, his opponent, 4.325. This poll was a very heavy one.being over 85 per cent.of the electorate. There were 810 more votes recorded than in 1887, and of these Mr. Brunner obtained 468 and Mr. Whiteley 342.

The organisation of the Liberals under the direc- tion of the agent, Mr. W. Handley. is exceedingly complete, and in every district besides a sub-event there is a band of voluntary workers who are work- ing incessantly.

The inhabitants of Weston have for some time now been the sufferers from an intermittent water supply if the statements made by some' of them be true. From the tons of the letter read at the meet- ing of the Runcorn Rural Council on Tuesday which had been received from Mr. Marshall, secretary to the Runcorn Water Committee, it would sePtll that that body is anxious to give as good a supply as possible. But they do not altogether admit the inadequacy of the supply. The letter explained that at the time of the Inspector's visit for the purpose of his report, it was found that the supply was cut off for the purpose of repairs. This, however, was flatly contradicted by Mr. Aaron who has taken an active part in ventilating the grievance ; a griev- ance which he contends still exists. The Inspector was instructed to make a second visit which it is to he hoped—even for the reputation of the Runcorn Water Committee—will he of a more satisfactory, character than the one he made a few weeks ago.

LIBER kL MEETING AT_ HAYDOCK,

A meeting in support of the candidature of Mr. James Moon was held at the New Boston, flaydock, on Tues- day evening. Councillor Turner presided, and he was supported by Mr. G. W. Rawlins, Mr. Walkden, miner's agent, Dr. Dowling, and Councillor Riley of Blackburn. After a brief introduction by the chairman, Mr. Moon asked on what grounds did he ask them to support the Liberal party. He would put it as plainly as he could. The Liberal party was a labour party, but he weeld not ask them to accept his authority for that. He believed Mr. Sam WO•Ci'S name was known in that district. Mr. Woods had said that the late Government was the best Labour Government that had ever been in power in this country, rue would refer them to Mr. Decay. the leader of the Labour Union, who said the action of the Government for Railway Servants had created nothing short of a revolution in the railway life. Who had brought the eight hours' day, years nearer operatioe ? It was the Liberal Government which had introduced the eight hours'day to all these servants. Who was it that had enabled every man in this country to claim free education for his child, without the stigma of poverty. The Conservatives claim that they gave them free education, but it was with the word pauperism written over :he school door. The Liberal Government had now enabled every working-man to claim free edu- cation as a matter of right, and it was the Liberal party they would have to look to. to bring about those mea- sures of reform which they were all wanting. He be- lieved there were forces at work now, which were grow- ing stronger every year, and which would ultimately make this country the brightest, happiest, most sober and prosperous country under the sun, and if they were true to their &Alpe now, they would support that party which gave them these results. (Hear, hear.) He asked them not to be led away by any false issues, to use their own judgment, and to vote irrespective of party, for the man they considered the best. They were told that Liberals were responsible for bad trade. Trade was never worse than when the Conservative Government went out of power in 1892. Whilst the Conservatives were in office the biggest failure that had ever taken place in this country happened. Baring Bros., went to the wall during the Conservative Government, and the Bank of England had come to their rescue (A Voice " Was trade ever as had as it is now ?") It was infinitely worse. RP was quite willing to agree with hie friend, that trade had been very bad in that district ; he quite agreed with him that the collier had not had sufficient work, and why was that? Simply because there were many more colliers than there is work for. It was not because more coat was being raised. The reason why they were nut of work and had short hours was not because trade was had, but because men were drafted into the mines, simply because there was no employment for them elsewhere. The Liberal Government by reenlating the hours of railway servants had enabled 10.000 more men to he employed. Heaven touched briefly on the Irish question, and passed on to the House of Lords. Did they think Mr.Legh was likely to pnsh their interests, if so what proof had he given of it ?- Three weeks ago he did not know what the Tore policy was. where had he eot it from ? It had been supplied to ruder by the Joseph of many colours. Vote for the , man whom they t honght most likely to advance the in- terests of the masses of the country. He appealed to them because they had a fearful responsibility. Their wee had as much weight as that, of a duke, and if they did not vote at the coming eleetinn,they were traitors to the cause. Vote, and do not he led off the scent by drink supplied at the public-houses by either party. (,) polause ) Mr. Dowline moved " That thie meeting of Haydock Liberal. comi-lers Mr. Moon a fit and proper person to represent the Newton Division in the House of Commons, and Wedges itself to use all lawful means to /tenure his re- tern at the cominmPlACtiritl." He thought that was a resnliitinn which should he carried unanimously. Re svceild advise them not to be lad away by Tory claptrap and humbug shout had trade. Tory Government in Havelnek generally had not improved the trade of Hay- dock. Mr. Legh was certainly not fit to represent a labour ronstituenry ; he would he right ennueh in a con- stituence of lawilords. Vote for Mr.Morm and they would get an Employers' Liability Act,without any contracting out. (Applause.) Mr. Walkden seconded the resolution in a speech of much force He said he did not believe there was a man in the whole of the United Kingdom who had done more to injure them as working-men. than Mr. Legh, who had voted against the miners having an eight hours' day. Tie believed that this election was being forght tan the iesue of bad trade. He thought it was time they as working-men ennsidered in what manner this bad trade had been brought, about. MEETING OF MR. MOON'S SUPPORTERS AT

SANKEY.

A mooting nf tho anopor,ora of Mr. Moon at Sankor 07Ag held on Wednesday nicht, on the Liverpool hi,rh road near to the church. The chair was occupied tu,

Mr. Gibbs, a local farmer, and there was a very good attendance,which after the commencement of the meet- ing was further augmented by a number of persons who came ha, 'tuts from Penketh, and also by a large number of cyclists who happened to he passing. Some of the latter at times showed their appreciation by the ringing of their bells. The Chairman, who stood in Mr. Moon's conveyance, which served AA a temporary platfQrm, said that there were tremendous issues to he decided by that election. He was sure that Mr. Moon, whom he was going to in- troduce to them, would give every satisfaction, and he hoped that he would he returned for the Newton divi- sion. He had been reading the nanera lately to find the Policy of the Tory party. (A Voice : "They have none.") He had come to the conclusion that that was the truth. (Flear, hear, and applawe ) The Tory policy was the policy of negation. Lord Salisbury's speeches reminded him of his school days, when some of his playmates had some toffee. They used to say, "Shut your eyes, and open your mouth, and Pee what God will send you." (Loud laughter end applause.) That was what Lord Ralistinry was asking. But if the people of England did what Lord Salishury asked them,his lordship might pop something in that was net very tasty, and then they would only have themselves to blame. (Applause 1 In conclusion, he asked his hearers to surport the Liberal party whose policy every one knew, and which was far the amelioration of the country. (Applaase.)

Mr.reloou, who was received with loud applause, and the singing of "tFor he's a jolly good fellow," said that he was sure that Mr. Gibbs would have made a good candidate for the division. He was glad to meet the electors of Saakey even in the open air. They would have plenty ef room to air their political opinions az all events. (Laughter.) They were told by their Tory op- ponents that although they (the Liberals) were fighting the Newton division, they would lose,but there were two sides to this as to every questiort, and they would be able to say more definitely how the Liberals stood in a week or two. (Hear, hear.) But whether they won or lost, they were lighting a good cause—applause—and it was to support that cause that he was asking them for their rates. If they would exercise their own opinions, if they would study the great questions of the day, they would come to the conclusion that there was only one party they coold support and that was the Liberal party. (Applause.) Every young man of twenty owed hiteducation to the Liberal party. They were going to exercise their vote. It was the Liberal party who gave that. (Applause.) The Liberal Government was re- turned to power three years ago with a certain pro- gramme. It died fighting for that programme,but it had never been defeated upon it. (Loud applause.) It was not defeated en a question of policy. It was defeated by a trick, not by the Conservatives, but by the bastard Liberals, who called themselves Liberal Unionists, who took the name of Liberal while they voted Tory. (Ap- plause). They were only Tories who had not the cour- age to say so. (Applause.) The Liberal Government had been the best Government that had ever existed on behalf of labour, and they had passed, or tried to pass many measures for the working classes. They had also established the eight hours in their workshops. They were told that the late Government had done nothing for agriculture. But what had the Tories done. (Nothing.) He would remind them that when a Bill was brought in to give compensation for disturbance, and for improvements upon farms and cottages, the Liberals to a man voted for it, while the Tories opposed it, and the man who was supposed to be their authority in agricultural matters—Mr. Chaplin—had to be shunted to another office which had nothing to do with agricul- ture because of his opposition to that Bill. (Loud apolause.) He had been asked whether he was an imper- ialist. He was an imperialist in the truest sense of the world, but he was no Jingo. (Applause.) Jingoism never made the country stronger, but on the contrary it did great injury to it He considered land reform and the abolition of royalties was very necessary, and that would do much to put an end to bad trade. There were men who were receiving as much money for royalties as the men received in wages—(shame)—and there were men who had aeres of land which they refused to sell or cultivate, and yet they contributed Practically nothing to the maintenance of the country. He did not ask his hearers to vote for himself, but for the cause he represented. The Liberal party was a steady friend of the democracy, and he hoped that on Monday week they would go to the poll and vote straight for the cause of social purity, social advancement, and political progress. (Loud applause.) Mr. Talbot moved a resolution pledging those present to vote for Mr. Moon at the forthcoming election. He said that Mr. Chamberlain some years ago declared that he would never give a blank cheque to Lord Salisbury, and new he was asking the country to give him one. He hoped that the country would refuse to give him any- thing of the kind—(applause)—hut would send the Lib- erals to power with a large majority. (Renewed ap- plause.) Dr. Thomas seconded the resolution. He said that be believed that every day that passed would increase Mr. Moon's chances of election, because he was sure that every time he addressed the electors he would carry con- viction to their minds. He was glad that the present battle was between Liberals and Tories, and that the hybrid party called the Unionists had disappeared. The Liberals welcomed the chance of appealing to the coun- try once more, and he was sure that the electors would declare against the domination of an irresponsible here- ditary chamber, and he appealed to them to return the Literals to power with a sufficient majority ito carry out the reforms to which they were pledged. (Applause.) The Liberals appealed to the country for freedom of the Howie of Commons from the contests of the other cham- ber. and he was sure that they would not appeal in vain. (A nplause.) The resolution was then put to the meeting and carried with only a very small number of dissentients. AIR. MOON AT PADGATE.

On Wednesday evening Mr. Moon, the Libere.i cand;- date for the Newton division, addressed a meeting of electors at Padget°. Mr. T Birchall, J.P.' presided,and there was a good attendance considering that the meet- ing was held at a rather inconvenient time—half-past six. _ _

The Chairman said that when asked to preside there that night he had some little hesitation about accepting the invitation as he was not a resident of Padgate,thoueh he must say it was not an unknown place to him nor had it been for the last fifty years. (Hear, hear.) But when he considered that his friends Aldermen Francomb and Sutton performed similar services for the Conservatives there, it was not out of place for him to come and do what he could for his friend Mr. Moon. (Applause.) With regard to the political situation, he said we had come to a great political crisis in our national history, and the people now had to choose between the two par- ties. The Tory party's promises were wonderful but rather vague, but on the other hand the Liberal party had something more than promises to show, as he con- sidered that the last three years of our Parliamentary history had been a remarkably good one. (Hear, hear, and applause.) He was now getting to be a pretty old politician, and he cOuld say that he had never known a Government which was so thoroughly honest and sin- cere, and he would say an thoroughly able as the Government which had just retiree] from office. (Hear, hear.) A good deal had been said about trade being bad while the Liberals were in power, but it was really a fact that dering the last Liberal administration the revenue returns showed that our exports and imports averaged a little over £2O a head of the population,while during the previous Tory administration they only aver- aged £l9. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. Moon on rising to speak met with a hearty re- ception. Thorne experienced the hope and belief that the electors of Padgatm and neighbourhood would ant according to their own judgment at the election, Mr. Moon said there could be no doubt that the Liberal party deserved well of the voter.; of the country. Nearly every extension of privileges to the masses had been given by the Liberal party. He referred to such questions as the ballot, compulsory education, extension of the fran- chise, the great Reform Bill, and the admission of Catholics to the House of Commons and Dissenters to the universities. (Applause ) All these measures had been opposed in the nest by the Conservative party and even to-day the late Liberal Government in its endeav- curs to extend the same privileges were opposed by the Conservative party just as they opposed in the nast. (Hear, hear.) As to foreign: policy he could s w that he was an imperialist is the cents that he desired to see the name and fame and pavver of this country of ours ex- tended, but he also believed in a domestic policy and he did not think the strength of England sprang entirely from its armed forces twine mainly from the extension of self-Government. (Applause.) Dealing with the work oF the late government heshowed the need for the value of the Employers' Liability Bill by mention- ing that in the course of the construction of theManches- tee Shin Canal no fewer than 2.500 men were killed and nnlv 25 of them got any compensation whatever. ("Shame.") Every rear there were 6,000 workmen killed by accident. All the Liberal party wanted to do was to provide that where an employer was in any way responsible for an accident the injured party should have compensation, vet the TIOUSS ef Lords threw the Billout. (Hear, hear.) Referring to the temperance question, Mr. Moon said his opnottent, Mr. Legh, had put temper- ance reform in his address, hut he (Mr. Moon) did not think that Mr. Leeh knew what temperance reform was. (Rear, hear, and laughter ) He had hen told that Mr. Leeh was asked at a mewing the night before what he meant by temperance reform,and after a little hesitation he reolied that he would answer the question on another occasion. (Laughter and applause.) At the conclusion of his address, which had been very well received, Mr. Moon had to leave the meeting to speak to other gatherings on the same evening, and as he left the room he was loudly applauded. Other addresses were also given, and an encouraging meeting was brought to a close by a vote of thanks to the Chairman.

MR. MOON AT PENKETR.

After addressing the meeting at Padgate on Wednes- day night, Mr. Moon proceeded to another meeting at Penketh. This meeting was held outside the Wesleyan schoolroom, and was presided aver by Mr. J. B. Aitken. There was a good number present. Mr.Aitken briefly addressed the meeting and said that he hoped that the electors of Penketh would do their duty on the election day. and that Mr. Moon would be rehirned at the head of the poll. (Applause.) Dr. Thomas, of Liverpool. then addressed the meet- ing. He criticised the various speeches of the leaders of the Tory party, and urged his hearers to do their utmost to defeat the party which Was the defender of privilege and of landlordism. and to return again tai power the 1 Liberal party which supported the claims of the working men. (Annlause.) Mr. C. E. Parker said that he had already taken part in four elections in the Newton division, and that was the fifth. It Was rather a remarkable thing that though the Liberal party had been defeated four times, they I came up again smiling as if nothing had ever happened. (Laughter and applause ) But althmagh they had been 1 beaten in the Newton division, still their principles bad triumphed all along the line. (Applause.) Take some of the measures which had become law. Take free edu- cation. When that question Was first brought forward, the Tories opposed it with all their might. They said "the idea of bachelors and those who had no children being called 'iron to pay for the education of somebody else's children." (Laughter.) And yet a Conservative Government came in and brought in free education. That was the way with the Tories. They opposed reform until they raw that the country was demanding it and than they brought in the measure they had been an much °noosed to. Then there was the Parish Councils Bill. This was one of the most . useful Bills that had been carried by the Literal party,. Mr. Parker then went on to read various amendments to that Bill for the o bject 0

daitroying its usefulnees. and which had been supported by Mr. Legh. He asked whether they were justified in again returning Mr.Legh to Parliament in the face of his past record. Referring to Home Rule for Ireland, Mr. Parker said that that question at the present time occu- pied a stronger position than ever it did before, and he was glad to see that the nationalities of Scotland and Wales were asking for local control. The Tory party were now claiming that they had passed the Factory Act. The Bill was, however, brought in by the Liberals, and it was owing to them that it had been successfully brought through. They must not forget that a few months ago, Mr. Lee' sneered at this Bill which the Tories now desired to get the credit of, and said that it would not benefit a single industry. He, Mr. Parker, had not seen Mr. Legies name associated with any progressive movement that had been brought into Parliament, since he went there, and the only ques- tion which seemed to agitate him was that of Liberal gentlemen being put upon the magistrate's bench in Lancashire. (Laughter.) The only thing he had done ler the Newton division was to give a few cups for bowling. (Laughter.) Bowling was a very good game, but it was not politics,and they wanted something more than that from their member. (Hear, hear.) He was convinced that by sending Mr. filoon in the place of Mr. Legh, they would be doing the right thing tor Penketh, and be hoped that on the polling day, they would have Mr. Moon as their member. (Applause.) Mr. Moon's principles were honest, and he would not make promises tor the sake of catching votes. (Loud applause.) Mr. White proposed a resolution pledging the meeting to use every legitimate means to secure Mr. Moon to Parliament as member for the Newton division. He said that at the meeting of the 300 at Earlestown, there was an absolute unanimity. He did not believe that because they were in a minority last time,they must give in. They must keep at it until they won. (Loud applause.) They were Englishmen, and Englishmen did not know wnen they were beaten. (Applause.) He was prepared to stand by Mr. Moon, as he believed that his, Mr. Moan's principles would tend to the social progress of the coun- try. (Applause.) Mr. Longshaw briefly seconded the resolution, and the chairman before putting it to the meeting called • upon

Mr. Moon who had a very cordial reception. He said that he might as well say at once that he stood by every line of his address. He did not put forward any per- sonal claims on their support. If he did not think the policy he believed in was entitled to their support, he would not certainly ask it on any other account. He did not wish to compare himself and his opponent Mr. Legh, because he might be a very estimable gentleman, and owned a great many thousands of acres. He (the speaker) could not claim that, but it was not his fault. (Laughter.) He appealed to their judgment, and to their commonsense to vote for the man whom they I thought to be the right man. (Applause.) He was quite sure that if the electors of Penketh would study the question, they would come to the conclusion that that man who would support the Liberal party was the right man for them. Only a few weeks ago Mr. Legh said openly that he did not know what the Conservative policy was. Now they had his address which was fur- nished complete and supplied wholesale from Birming- ham, at Birmingham rates. (Laughter and applau.e.) Were they going to take the Tories at their valuation. (Hear, hear.) The man that produced that policy for Mr. Lagh was Mr. Joseph Chamberlain, of many colours (Laughter.) He (Mr. Moon) believed that the present election was one of the most momentous they had ever had, and he believed that there were forces working in the country which would make England the best and most sober and most prosperous country in the world. (Applause.) If they would look at Mr. Legh's address, they would find that all the reforms that he men- tioned were stolen clothes of the Liberal party, and they must remember that stolen clothes never fit the wearer as well as those for whom they were made. (Ap- plause). lie contended that the late Government had been the most democratic government of modern times, and never had so much been done for the working classes The Liberal party was the party which would do the most for the agriculturalist. How was it that farmers and agricultural labourers voted for the Tory candidate? (A Voice : " Because they known no better, and laugh- ter.) The Tory party always opposed everything which was for the benefit of the farmer, and it was not to be expected that a party of landlords would do much for the tenants. He fought that battle because he was con- vinced that the people of this country were prepared to support popular rights against hereditary priviiege (ap- plause), and the greatest good for the greatest number. He also stood on the temperance platform which he be- lieved would raise the standard of the people. He sin- cerely trusted that on Monday week, his hearers would exercise their votes in favour of the Liberal party who had given them the privilege of the vote. (Loud ap- plause.) ..

The resolution of confidence was then submitted, and was passed unanimously. Mr. G. W. Rawlins, J.P., proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman and the proceedings then terminated. Mr. J. Moon drove away amid loud chears,to address another meeting at Sankey._

MOON FOR NEWTON.

Newton electors one and all, Your clouds will disappear If you for Moon will give your vote Hell shine out bright and dear. In other spheres he has shone bright And gently cleared the sky, So kindly note, give him your vote, Your clouds will then roll by. Your interests in his hands are safe. He every nerve will bend ; To elevate his fellow man A helping hand will lend. Those measures he will advocate Which benefit mankind ; Most surely in this eandidste A champion bold you'll find. So rally round this noble man And solid go for Moon ; A change in this division We cannot have too soon. An active man is needed here And for him we will fight ; The Tory cloud we must remove And have Moon clear and bright. The prospects will be bright for all ; In Moon we'll have a friend ; So let us note, and for Lim vote, Our man we cannot mend.

HAL-TON. LADIES CLUB ANNIVIDISARY.—ThgT9th anniversary of the Halton Female Friendly Society was celebrated on Monday. The members assembled at the schoolroom,and forming into processional order proceeded to the Castle Hotel Green. After parading round the green three times in accordance with ancient custom, the processionists proceeded to the Parish Church, when a service was con- ducted by the Rev. S. Wilkinson. Afterwards the pro- cession was re-formed, and headed by the Runcorn Old Town Band. under the conductorship of Mr. J. Woods, they paraded the village. In the proce.sion there wero Lady Brooke. Mrs. Wray Rev. G. D. Wray and Rev. S. Wilkinson, Mr. Charles Reynolds, J.P., Mr. Thomas Wright, Mr. Gamon and about 200 others. An excellent tea was provided in a large marquee provided by, Mrs. .inipson.—After tea Mrs. Wray proposed a vote of thanks to Lady Brooke which was carried. The trustees Messrs. C. Reynolds and John Henry Gamon, and the secretary. Mr. 'P. Wright, were also thanked for their services. Dancing on the Green was afterwards indul- ged in.

EARLESTOWN WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL.

The young men's select class in connection with the above school have introduced a system by which they can provide themselves with useful and instructive books. Three months ago 25 of them decided to subscribe weekly a certain amount for the purpose of purchasing books, and on Sunday, the Rev. J. Bartley after delivering a short address on " books and readings," presented the books which had been obtained, to the subscribers. The innovation will be continued, and it is expected the young ladies will also take up the idea. NEWTON 1.0.G.T. EBENEZER LODGE.

Ross QUERN FESTIVAL AT HAYDOCIL In beautiful weather on Saturday afternoon, the first annual festival in connection with the coron- ation of the Rose Queen was held on the Haydock Football Field. For several years past a festival has been held in connection with the crowning of the May Queen, but for some reason or other the matter was not taken up sufficiently early this year for the celebration to be repeated, and it was,there- fore decided to instal a " Rose Queen." The dignity of Queen was balloted for as in the May festival,and the lot fell upon Miss B. Waldron, of Lime-st.,llay- dock. There did not appear to be the mime entha- iasm manifested in this celebration, as on previous occasions, and the attendance of the public in the streets on the route of the procession was not at any time large, but this may very easily be ac- counted for by the fact that there were counter at- tractions in the neighbourhood. The processionists mustered at the football field, and after something like order had been obtained, they moved off, with ' Mr. S. Pimblott as marshall, in the direction of the Huntsman Inn, returning by the same route as far as the Ram's Head, and back to the field, the time occupied being nearly thres hours. The procession although not of very large dimensions was extreniely neat, and excellent order was maintained. The 3haracters were not numerous, but they were select and the dresses of the dancers, &c., were far in ad- vance of any previous year. The Haydock brass band held the premier position, and were followed by the floral dancers,Robin Hood.and a capital illus. tration of a white female slave on a lurry, tied to a stake,with a cruel oversesr armed with a whip.with which he is supposed to have lashed the unfortun- ate slave. Then followed sailors, Buffalo Bill,flower girls, the seasons, day and night, Robinson Crusoe, bakers, Spanish lady and gentleman, lawn tennis players, little 80-peep and Dick Whittington, scarf and wand drill performers, Darby and Joan, Red Riding Hood, Alley Sloper, and a non descript band of characters, with gigantic masks, dress- makers, teachers, carriage with ex-May Queen, sol- diers, clown and pantaloon, A. I. Bobby, lady hun- ters, Nutgrove brass band, May Pole dancers, and a number of other characters including, of course, a specimen of "The New Woman." The carriage containing the Roe Queen was attended by an escort of husqars,and the rear was brought up by a ventriloquist with his dolls. On arrival at the field, and after the usual interval, the Queen was crowned by Miss Balharry, who presented her with a Cat'iolic service as a memento of the occasion. Then followed the May Pole dance under the super- vision of Mr. J. Ralphs, scarf drill by children from th. National Schools who had been trained by Mr. J. Puffer, floral dance, physical drill, under the supervision of Miss Sawyer, champion boxers and knockabouts, wand drill, and 'ventriloquial enter• tainment by Professor D. Ora. The whole of the arrangements were carried out by a large committee of which Mr. R. T. Kenyon as secretary and Mr. S. Pimblett treasurer, the M.O 's being Messrs. H Worsley and J. Ralphs. Before the procession started prizes were awarded for the neatest and best turnouts as follows : eavy class, Ist., 2nd., and 3rd, Messrs. R. Evans and Co. ; light class, Ist, Mr. T. Harrison; 2nd, Dr. Hayward ; 3rd, Mr. J. Travis.

HELSBY.

TAB NEW VICAR.—The Rev. A. Spafford, who has been preferred to the Vicarage of St. Paul's Church, Helsby, was instituted to the living on Saturday afternoon, by the Bishop of Chester, who delivered a very appropriate address. His Lordship spoke ef the responsibilities of the pastor and of the Church people of the village, and how it was necessary for one to assist the other in the fulfilment of the dullea. His lordship raid a passing tribute to the geniality and liberal heartedness of the Rev. W. T. Vale. who had formerly filled the position in a way that gained for him the respect of those among whom he laboured.
