TO THE ELECTORS OF THE NORTHWICH DIVISION. GENTLEMEN. For the fifth time within ten years solicit the honour of Election to serve you in the Commons House of Parliament Three times you have elected me by steadily increasing majorities. Once you preferred a gentleman, who up to that time had been a consistent Liberal, and who had lived an honoured life among you. I can Cahn therefore that this is a thoroughly Liberal constituency I have as your representative been faithful to every pledge I have given to you. Ten years ago—in my Address— I set before myself two aims for your benefit, non-political in their nature ; the first, the reform of the Weaver Navigation Trust, and the second, an amendment of the law which should procure compensation for property owners suffering from the inevit- able injury caused by the pumping of brine. In 1891 I introduced a Bill providing for compensation for Subsidence, which, after amendment in Committee, became law. In 1893—through the kind help of Members on both sides of the House—l obtained an order from Parliament to the Weaver Trustees that within two years they should bring up a Bill for the Reformatien'of their own Constitution. This year, in obedience to that Order, the Weaver Trustees brought up their Bill, and I brought up a Bill of my own. MV Bill, with the unanimous assent of the Weaver Trustees, the County Cowen, and the Traders, was adopted by the Trus- tees in place if their own ; and with very slight alteration, was passed by both Houses, and received the Royal Assent. A Committee of the House of Commons has. quite recently, adopted a Bill constituting a District within which the Com- -1 pensation Act I procured in 1891, will. I trust, shortly be put into operation. The new Representative Managing Body of the Weaver Navigation will doubtless speedily complete the great scheme of improvement of the River (and especially of the upper part) now so long neglected, and will by the adoption of an enterprising progressive policy, attract trade to our neighbour- hood, and thus greatly add to the prosperity of the popula- tion, land owners, traders, and workmen alike, from end to end of the constitueney—from Winsford to Runcorn. The payment of compensation for subsidence will he an act of justice greatly too long delayed. I make my appeal to you now in a spirit of cheerful satis- faction with the success of my many years of work and thought. My political views are wall-known to you ; they have not changed, except that with advancing years they become and more comprehensive. Now that our venerated leader, Mr. Gladstone, has said his dignified word of farewell t Parliamentary office,we recall to mind his great and memo ra speech on the subject of the House of Ltrds. Standing in his place as Leader of the House of Commons and Prime Minister, in Match last year, Mr. Gladstone declared In the name of a unanimous Govern- ment, that a state of things had been reached of which we are compelled to say that in ourjudgment it cannot continue. "In some way or other a solution will have to be found for this tremendous contrariety and incessant conflict on matters of high principle and profound importance between the re- presentatives of the people and those who fill an hereditary chamber. Nigh upon two hundred years ago we abolished the Veto of the Crown. That was not Revolution, but Reform. I stand before you to-day as a strenuous and ardent advo- cate of the abolition of the veto of the House of Lords, not as a Revolutionist but as a consistent Reformer. 1 Upon this greatly to be desired ieform depend all others— Home Rule for all the three Kingdoms and for Wales.— Reform of the Land Laws, involving security for the farmer and for every other trading tenant,—the transfer of the Power to Regulate and Control the Traffic in Intoxicating Liquors from the hands of irresponsible magistrates into those of the people themselves—Protection of Life and Health for Work-people—equality of Voting Power in the election of National Representatives—Disestablishment of the Welsh Church, and all measures having for their object the improve- ment of the social condition of the people. It has been my happy lot for over a score of years to share in every effort you have made for the public good. You have been kind to me in my sorrows, you have sym- pathised in my good fortune. In return I have given to you devoted service, and I desire nothing more strongly than the opportunity to continue that service. I remain, Gentlemen. Your faithful servant, JOI-IN TOMLINSON BRUNNER. Winnington Old Hall, Northwich, 6th July, 1895. TO THE ELECTORS OF TUE WIDNES DIVISION OF SOUTH-WEST LANCASHIRE. GENTLEMEN, In response to the unanimous request of the District Liberal Councils I have consented to again become the Liberal Candidate for the Widnes Division. It is for you to consider and determine whether the work done by the Liberal Party while in power, and the spirit shewn, entitle the Party to your support, and also whether I have done anything to increase or to decrease the confidence which so many Electors reposed in me as a Candidate three years ago. The main question submitted at the last Election was that of Home Rule for Ireland. Th. Liberal Government brought in a Bill in accordance with the decision arrived at by the Electors of the United Kingdom as a whole. The Bill pro- vided for the Establehnent of a Home Rule Government in Ireland, while it absolutely preserved the Supremacy of the Imperial Parliament. It vent passed by the House of Commons but was rejected by the House of Lords. I deplore the fact that this and other Bills concerned with Irish Affairs hove not been passed into Law, but this has been owing to no fault of the Liberal Government, which has been strenuously and loyally supported by the Majority of the Irish Members. If elected, I shall give my support to the furtherance of the Policy initiated by Mr. Gladstone. The Liberal Government passedin Law the PARISH COUN- CILS' ACT, which has given to the Inhabitants of the Rural Townships the Right to ldan%ge their own Affairs. It also passed the Great 13udget of 1894, which Equalised und Gradu- ated the Death Duties en Real and Personal Property, and put extra Taxation on to the shoulders of the wealthier classes to the direct and immediate relief of the less wealthy. The Factory Act, introduced by-the Liberal Government has been passed into Law. The Employers' Liability Bill was lost by the action of the House of Lords. The "Local Veto" Bill was introduced, and met with the cordial approval of Temperance Reformers. When the Liberal Government resigned, a Bill for the Di. establishment and Disendowment of the Church in Wales, and a Land Bill for Ireland were under discussion in the House of Commons. In all Departments of Administration, the LIBERAL GOVERNMENT has accomplished admirable work,and there has not been in this Country any Government so Democratic as the last. The National Defences have been strengthened, and our Foreign Affairs have been skilfully directed. In addition to the q estions already referred to, there are still Reforms to be effected in the Registration Laws, and we cannot be content until Plural Voting has been abolished It , I. essentially the duty of Parliament to try, as far as is pos- sible by Legislation, to further ameliozate the conditions of Labour, to Remove the Causes of Poverty, and to Lighten the Burdens of the Aged Poor. Efforts to further these aims have always had my sympathy, and to them, If elected, I shall devote especial attention It is of vital importance that the land should be made as productive as possible, and to secure this, there must be wide - reaching Reforms of the existing Land Laws, while Farmers must be able to recover just Compensation for their Improve- ments. There is, however, one permanent obstacle to the passing of Liberal legislation into law. During the life of the late Parliament, the House of Lords has exercised its veto in the manner it always does 'when a Liberal Government is in office. The absolute power which the unrepresentative House of Lords now possesses, to over-ride the decisions of the elected House of Commons, has become intolerable, and I think that the "legislative preponderance" of the House of Lords must be abolished. If you honour me by electing me to represent you in the next Parliament, I shall, to the best of my ability, support measures which I believe will promote the welfare of our country, while at all times I shall devote willing attention to matters affecting the interests of the Electors of the Widnes Division. I am, Gentlemen, Your Obedient Servant, HENRY WADE DEACON. Appleton House, Applet n, Widnes, July 6th, 1t95.

WEEKLY CHAT.—Subject: Second-hand Furniture.— The opening of Spring is the time for the busy house- wife to put her house in order, therefore the matter of furni- shing must be well discussed. We cannot, therefore, do better than advise a visit to Worsley's before definitely de- riding where and what to purchase. This visit cannot pos- sibly but prove remunerative, if only in experience. "The Pantheon " being a hive of industry, it is a palatial pile of buildings close to the Central Station, having a frontage ot nearly 150 feet This immense building is filled from floor to ceiling (six storeys) with the grandest collection of High- class Second-hand Furniture in the World. This is not an exaggeration, but can be verified by ocular olemonstrat ion if you pay a visit to Manchester. Suites of Bedroom Furni- ture, for instance, by such makers as Goodall. Lamb, Ogden, ece., original coet from £2OO to £3OO, for from £5O to £7O ; suites, original cost £5O to £7O, for £lO to £2O. Pay a visit and see for yourself ; you can surely use your common sense, and see if Worsley's is the place as described.
